{(c_{3},\mathit{out},c_{1},\mathit{in})}
\mathsf{H}
\mathsf{T}
x,c_{1}
z,c_{3}
\mathsf{T}
\mathsf{H}
x,c_{1}
z,c_{3}
{(c_{3},\mathit{out},c_{1},\mathit{in})}
\mathsf{H}
\mathsf{T}
x,c_{1}
z,c_{3}
\mathsf{T}
\mathsf{H}
x,c_{1}
z,c_{3}
{(c_{3},\mathit{out},c_{1},\mathit{in})}
{(c_{1},\mathit{out},c_{3},\mathit{in})}
\mathsf{disconnect}(x.\mathit{out},y.\mathit{in})
\mathsf{disconnect}(y.\mathit{out},z.\mathit{in})
\mathsf{delete}(y)
\mathsf{connect}(x.\mathit{out},z.\mathit{in})

A configuration is changed by two types of
actions
: (a)

havoc
actions change the local states of the components by
executing interactions (that trigger simultaneous transitions in
different components), without changing the structure or the store,
and (b)
reconfiguration
actions that change the structure,
store and possibly the state map of a configuration. We refer to
Fig.
2
for a depiction of havoc and
reconfiguration actions. Each havoc action is the result of executing
a sequence of interactions (horizontally depicted using straight
double arrows), whereas each reconfiguration action (vertically
depicted using snake-shaped arrows) corresponds to a statement in a
reconfiguration program. The two types of actions may interleave,
yielding a transition graph with a finite but unbounded (parametric)
or even infinite (obtained by iteratively adding new components) set
of vertices (configurations).

Formally, an
action
is a function
f:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{pow}({\Gamma})^{\top}
, where
\mathrm{pow}({\Gamma})^{\top}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\mathrm{pow}({\Gamma})\cup\{{\top}\}
. The complete lattice

(\mathrm{pow}({\Gamma}),\subseteq,\cup,\cap)
is extended with a greatest
element
\top
, with the conventions
S\cup\top\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\top
and
S\cap\top\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}S
, for each
S\in\mathrm{pow}({\Gamma})
. We consider that an action
f
is
disabled

in a configuration
\gamma
if and only if
f(\gamma)=\emptyset

and that it
faults
in
\gamma
if and only if
f(\gamma)=\top
. Actions are naturally lifted to sets of configurations
as
f(S)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\bigcup_{\gamma\in S}f(\gamma)
, for each
S\subseteq\Gamma
.

Definition 3

The
havoc
action
\mathfrak{h}:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{pow}({\Gamma})
is defined as
\mathfrak{h}(\gamma)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\{{\gamma^{\prime}\mid\gamma\xRightarrow{{\scriptstyle}}_{\raisebox{4.0pt}{\!$\scriptstyle{}$}}^{*}\gamma^{\prime}}\}
, where

\xRightarrow{{\scriptstyle}}_{\raisebox{4.0pt}{\!$\scriptstyle{}$}}^{*}
is the reflexive and transitive closure of the
relation
\xRightarrow{{\scriptstyle}}_{\raisebox{4.0pt}{\!$\scriptstyle{}$}}~\subseteq\Gamma\times\Gamma
,
defined by the following rule:

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}(c_{1},p_{1},c_{2},p_{2})\in\mathcal{I}&c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathcal{C}&\varrho(c_{i})=q_{i}&q_{i}\xrightarrow{{\scriptstyle p_{i}}}q^{\prime}_{i},&\text{for all }i=1,2\end{array}

(
\mathsf{Havoc}
)

(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)\xRightarrow{{\scriptstyle}}_{\raisebox{4.0pt}{\!$\scriptstyle{}$}}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho[c_{1}\leftarrow q^{\prime}_{1}][c_{2}\leftarrow q^{\prime}_{2}],\nu)

Note that the havoc action is the result of executing any sequence of
tight interactions, whereas loose interactions are simply ignored.
The above definition can be generalized to multi-party interactions

(c_{1},p_{1},\ldots,c_{n},p_{n})
with
n\geq 1
pairwise distinct
participant components
c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}
, that fire simultaneously
transitions of the behavior labeled with the ports
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
,
respectively. In particular, the interactions of arity
n=1

correspond to the local (silent) actions performed independently by a
single component. To keep the presentation simple, we refrain from
considering such generalizations, for the time being.

Example 3

Let
\gamma_{i}=(\{{c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}}\},\{{(c_{i},\mathit{out},c_{i~\mathrm{mod}~3+1},\mathit{in})\mid i\in[{1},{3}]}\},\varrho_{i},\nu)
, for
i\in[{1},{3}]
be the top-most
configurations from Fig.
2
, where

\varrho_{1}(c_{1})=\varrho_{1}(c_{2})=\mathsf{H}
,
\varrho_{1}(c_{3})=\mathsf{T}
,
\varrho_{2}(c_{1})=\mathsf{T}
,
\varrho_{2}(c_{2})=\varrho_{2}(c_{3})=\mathsf{H}
,
\varrho_{3}(c_{1})=\varrho_{3}(c_{3})=\mathsf{H}
,
\varrho_{3}(c_{2})=\mathsf{T}
and
\nu(x)=c_{1}
,

\nu(y)=c_{2}
,
\nu(z)=c_{3}
. Then
\mathfrak{h}(\gamma_{i})=\{{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}}\}
, for all
i\in[{1},{3}]
.
\blacksquare

2.1
The Expressiveness of the Model

Before moving on with the definition of a logic describing sets of
configurations (§
3
), a reconfiguration language and a
proof system for reconfiguration programs (§
4
), we
discuss the expressive power of the

components-behavior-interactions
model of distributed systems
introduced so far, namely
what kinds of distributed algorithms
can be described in our model
?

On one hand, this model can describe message-passing algorithms on
networks with unrestricted topologies (pipelines, rings, stars, trees,
grids, cliques, etc.), such as flooding/notification of a crowd,
token-based mutual exclusion, deadlock problems (dining
philosophers/cryptographers), etc. Moreover, the model captures
asynchronous communication, via bounded message channels modeled using
additional components
4
4

4

The number of messages in transit
depends on the number of states in the behavior; unbounded message
queues would require an extension of the model to infinite-state
behaviors.
. Furthermore,
transient faults
(process delays,
message losses, etc.) can be modeled as well, by nondeterministic
transitions e.g., a channel component might chose to
nondeterministically lose a message. In particular, having a single
finite-state machine that describes the behavior of all components is
not a limitation, because finitely many behaviors
\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{m}
can be represented by state machines with disjoint transition
graphs, the state map distinguishing between different behavior types
– if
\varrho(c)=q
and
q
is a state of
\mathcal{B}_{i}
, the value of

\varrho(c)
can never change to a state of a different behavior

\mathcal{B}_{j}
, as the result of a havoc action.

On the other hand, the current model cannot describe complex
distributed algorithms, such as leader election

[
21
,
29
]
, spanning tree

[
60
,
46
]
, topological linearization

[
39
]
, Byzantine consensus

[
48
]
or Paxos parliament

[
47
]
, due to the following
limitations:

•

Finite-state behavior is oblivious of the identity of the
components (processes) in distributed systems of arbitrary
sizes. For instance, there is no distributed algorithm over rings
that can elect a leader under the assumption of anonymous processes

[
21
,
29
]
.

•

Interactions between a bounded number of participants cannot
describe broadcast between arbitrarily many components, as in most
common consensus algorithms

[
48
,
47
]
.

We proceed in the rest of the paper under these simplifying
assumptions (i.e., finite-state behavior and bounded-arity
interactions), as our focus is modeling the reconfiguration aspect of
a distributed system, and consider the following extensions for future
work:

•

Identifiers in registers
: the behavior is described by a
finite-state machine equipped with finitely many registers
r

holding component identifiers, that can be used to send (
p!r
) and
receive (
p?r
) identifiers (
p
stands for a port name), perform
equality (
r=r^{\prime}
) and strict inequality (
r<r^{\prime}
) checks, with no
other relation or function on the domain of identifiers. For
instance, identifier-aware behaviors are considered in

[
2
]
in the context of bounded model
checking i.e., verification of temporal properties (safety and
liveness) under the assumption that the system has a ring topology
and proceeds in a bounded number of rounds (a round is completed
when every component has executed exactly one
transition). Algorithms running on networks of arbitrary topologies
(described by graphs) are modeled using
distributed register
automata

[
8
]
, that offer a promising
lead for verifying properties of distributed systems with mutable
networks.

•

Broadcast interactions
: interactions involving an
unbounded number of component-port pairs e.g., the
p_{0}
ports of
all components except for a bounded set
c_{1},\ldots,c_{k}
, that
interact with ports
p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}
, for a given integer constant

k\geq 0
. Broadcast interactions are described using universal
quantifiers in
[
10
]
, where network
topologies are specified using first-order logic. To accomodate
broadcast interactions in our model, one has to redefine
composition, by considering e.g., glueing of interactions, in
addition to the disjoint union of configurations
(Def.
2
). Changing this definition would have a
non-trivial impact on the configuration logic used to write
assertions in reconfiguration proofs (§
3
).

Considering a richer model of behavior (e.g., register automata, timed
automata, or even Markov decision processes) would impact mainly the
part of the framework that deals with checking the properties (i.e.,
safety, liveness or havoc invariance) of a set of configurations
described by a formula of the configuration logic (defined in
§
3
) but should not, in principle, impact the
configuration logic itself, the programming language or the proof
system for reconfiguration programs (defined in
§
4
). However, accomodating broadcast communication
requires changes at the level of the logic and, consequently, the
reconfiguration programs proof system.

3
A Logic of Configurations

We define a
Configuration Logic
(
CL
) that is, an assertion
language describing sets of configurations. Let
\mathbb{A}
be a
countably infinite set of predicate symbols, where
\#(\mathsf{A})\geq 1

denotes the arity of a predicate symbol
\mathsf{A}\in\mathbb{A}
. The
CL
formulæ are inductively described by the following
syntax:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\phi&::=&\mathsf{true}\mid\mathsf{emp}\mid x=y\mid{x}@{q}\mid\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle\mid\mathsf{A}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\#(\mathsf{A})})\mid\phi*\phi\mid\phi\wedge\phi\mid\neg\phi\mid\exists x~.~\phi\end{array}

where
q\in Q
,
\mathsf{A}\in\mathbb{A}
are predicate symbols and

x,y,x_{1},x_{2},\ldots\in\mathbb{V}
are variables. The atomic formulæ

{x}@{q}
,
\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle
, and
\mathsf{A}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\#(\mathsf{A})})
are called
component
,

interaction
and
predicate atoms
, respectively. A formula
is said to be
predicate-free
if it has no occurrences of
predicate atoms. By
\mathrm{fv}({\phi})
we denote the set of free variables
in
\phi
, that do not occur within the scope of an existential
quantifier. A formula is
quantifier-free
if it has no
occurrence of existential quantifiers. A
substitution
is a
partial mapping
\sigma:\mathbb{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{V}
and the formula

\phi\sigma
is the result of replacing each free variable
x\in\mathrm{fv}({\phi})\cap\mathrm{dom}({\sigma})
by
\sigma(x)
in
\phi
. We denote by

[x_{1}/y_{1},\ldots,x_{k}/y_{k}]
the substitution that replaces
x_{i}
with

y_{i}
, for all
i\in[{1},{k}]
. We use the shorthands

\mathsf{false}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\neg\mathsf{true}
,
x\neq y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\neg x=y
,

\phi_{1}\vee\phi_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\neg(\neg\phi_{1}\wedge\neg\phi_{2})
,

\forall x~.~\phi_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\neg(\exists x~.~\neg\phi_{1})
and

{x}@{\_}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\bigvee_{q\in Q}{x}@{q}
.

We distinguish the boolean (
\wedge
) from the separating (
*
)
conjunction:
\phi_{1}\wedge\phi_{2}
means that
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}

hold for the same configuration, whereas
\phi_{1}*\phi_{2}
means that

\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
hold separately, on two disjoint parts of the
same configuration. Intuitively, a formula
\mathsf{emp}
describes empty
configurations, with no components and interactions,
{x}@{q}

describes a configuration with a single component, given by the store
value of
x
, in state
q
, and
\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle

describes a single interaction between ports
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
of the
components given by the store values of
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
,
respectively. The formula
{x_{1}}@{q_{1}}*\ldots*{x_{n}}@{q_{n}}*\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle*\ldots*\langle{{x_{n-1}}.{\mathit{p_{n-1}}},{x_{n}}.{\mathit{p_{n}}}}\rangle
describes a structure consisting
of
n

pairwise distinct
components, in states
q_{1},\ldots,q_{n}
, respectively, joined by interactions between ports
p_{i}
and

p_{i+1}
, respectively, for all
i\in[{1},{n-1}]
.

The
CL
logic is used to describe configurations of distributed
systems of unbounded size, by means of predicate symbols, defined
inductively by a given set of rules. For reasons related to the
existence of (least) fixed points, the definitions of predicates are
given in a restricted fragment of the logic. The
symbolic
configurations
are formulæ of the form
\xi\wedge\pi
, where
\xi
and
\pi
are defined by the
following syntax:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\xi::=\mathsf{emp}\mid{x}@{q}\mid\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle\mid\mathsf{A}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\#(\mathsf{A})})\mid\xi*\xi&&\pi::=x=y\mid x\neq y\mid\pi\wedge\pi\end{array}

The interpretation of
CL
formulæ is given by a semantic
relation
\models_{\Delta}
, parameterized by a finite
set of
inductive definitions
(SID)
\Delta
, consisting of rules
\mathsf{A}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\#(\mathsf{A})})\leftarrow\exists y_{1}\ldots y_{k}~.~\phi
,
where
\phi
is a symbolic configuration, such that
\mathrm{fv}({\phi})\subseteq\{{x_{1},\ldots,x_{\#(\mathsf{A})}}\}\cup\{{y_{1},\ldots,y_{k}}\}
. The relation
\models_{\Delta}
is defined inductively on the
structure of formulæ, as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rclcl}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)&\models_{\Delta}&\mathsf{true}&\iff&\text{true}\\
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)&\models_{\Delta}&\mathsf{emp}&\iff&\mathcal{C}=\emptyset\text{ and }\mathcal{I}=\emptyset\\
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)&\models_{\Delta}&x=y&\iff&\nu(x)=\nu(y)\\
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)&\models_{\Delta}&{x}@{q}&\iff&\mathcal{C}=\{{\nu(x)}\},~\mathcal{I}=\emptyset\text{ and }\varrho(\nu(x))=q\\
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)&\models_{\Delta}&\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle&\iff&\mathcal{C}=\emptyset,~\mathcal{I}=\{{(\nu(x_{1}),p_{1},\nu(x_{2}),p_{2})}\}\\
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)&\models_{\Delta}&\mathsf{A}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{\#(\mathsf{A})})&\iff&(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)\models_{\Delta}\varphi[x_{1}/y_{1},\ldots,x_{\#(\mathsf{A})}/y_{\#(\mathsf{A})}]\text{, for some}\\
&&&&\text{rule }\mathsf{A}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\#(\mathsf{A})})\leftarrow\varphi\text{ from $\Delta$}\\
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)&\models_{\Delta}&\phi_{1}*\phi_{2}&\iff&\text{there exist configurations }\gamma_{1}\text{ and }\gamma_{2},\text{ such that }\\
&&&&(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)=\gamma_{1}\bullet\gamma_{2}\text{ and }\gamma_{i}\models_{\Delta}\phi_{i}\text{, for both }i=1,2\\
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)&\models_{\Delta}&\phi_{1}\wedge\phi_{2}&\iff&(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)\models_{\Delta}\phi_{i}\text{, for both }i=1,2\\
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)&\models_{\Delta}&\neg\phi_{1}&\iff&\text{not }(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)\models_{\Delta}\phi_{1}\\
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)&\models_{\Delta}&\exists x~.~\phi_{1}&\iff&(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu[x\leftarrow c],\varrho)\models_{\Delta}\phi_{1}\text{, for some $c\in\mathbb{C}$}\end{array}

From now on, we consider the SID
\Delta
to be clear from the context
and write
\gamma\models\phi
instead of
\gamma\models_{\Delta}\phi
. If
\gamma\models\phi
, we say that
\gamma
is a

model
of
\phi
and define the set of models of
\phi
as

{{\bf[\![}\phi{\bf]\!]}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\{{\gamma\mid\gamma\models\phi}\}
. A
formula
\phi
is
satisfiable
if and only if
{{\bf[\![}\phi{\bf]\!]}}\neq\emptyset
. Given formulæ
\phi
and
\psi
, we say that

\phi
entails
\psi
if and only if
{{\bf[\![}\phi{\bf]\!]}}\subseteq{{\bf[\![}\psi{\bf]\!]}}
, written
\phi\models\psi
.

Example 4

The SID below defines chains of components and interactions, with at
least
h,t\in\mathbb{N}
components in state
\mathsf{H}
and
\mathsf{T}
,
respectively:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathsf{chain}_{{0},{1}}(x,x)\leftarrow{x}@{\mathsf{T}}&\mathsf{chain}_{{h},{t}}(x,y)\leftarrow\exists z.~{x}@{\mathsf{T}}*\langle{{x}.{\mathit{out}},{z}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*\mathsf{chain}_{{h},{t\dot{-}1}}(z,y)\\
\mathsf{chain}_{{1},{0}}(x,x)\leftarrow{x}@{\mathsf{H}}&\mathsf{chain}_{{h},{t}}(x,y)\leftarrow\exists z.~{x}@{\mathsf{H}}*\langle{{x}.{\mathit{out}},{z}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*\mathsf{chain}_{{h\dot{-}1},{t}}(z,y)\\
\mathsf{chain}_{{0},{0}}(x,x)\leftarrow{x}@{\_}\end{array}

where
k\dot{-}1\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\max(k-1,0)
, for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. The
configurations
(\{c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}\},\{(c_{i},\mathit{out},c_{(i~\mathrm{mod}~n)+1},\mathit{in})\mid i\in[{1},{n}]\},\varrho,\nu)
from Example
1
are models of the
formula
\exists x\exists y~.~\mathsf{chain}_{{0},{0}}(x,y)*\langle{{y}.{\mathit{out}},{x}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle
, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. This is because any
such configuration can be decomposed into a model of

\langle{{y}.{\mathit{out}},{x}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle
and a model of
\mathsf{chain}_{{0},{0}}(x,y)
. The
latter is either a model of
{x}@{\_}
, matching the body of the
rule
\mathsf{chain}_{{0},{0}}(x,x)\leftarrow{x}@{\_}
if
x=y
, or a
model of
\exists z.~{x}@{\_}*\langle{{x}.{\mathit{out}},{z}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*\mathsf{chain}_{{0},{0}}(z,y)
, matching the body of the rule
\mathsf{chain}_{{0},{0}}\leftarrow\exists z.~{x}@{\_}*\langle{{x}.{\mathit{out}},{z}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*\mathsf{chain}_{{0},{0}}(z,y)
etc.
\blacksquare

3.1
The Expressiveness of
CL

The
CL
logic is quite expressive, due to the interplay between
first-order quantifiers and inductively defined predicates. For
instance, the class of
cliques
, in which there is an
interaction between the
\mathit{out}
and
\mathit{in}
ports of any
two present components are defined by the formula:

\forall x\forall y~.~{x}@{\_}*{y}@{\_}*\mathsf{true}\rightarrow\langle{{x}.{\mathit{out}},{y}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*\mathsf{true}

Describing clique-structured
networks is important for modeling consensus protocols, such as
Byzantine
[
48
]
or Paxos

[
47
]
.

Connected
networks, used in e.g., linearization algorithms

[
38
]
, are such that there exists a path of interactions
between each two present components in the system:

\forall x\forall y~.~{x}@{\_}*{y}@{\_}*\mathsf{true}\rightarrow\mathit{reach}(x,y)

where the predicate
\mathit{reach}(x,y)
is
defined by the following rules:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathit{reach}(x,y)\leftarrow x=y,&\mathit{reach}(x,y)\leftarrow\exists z~.~{x}@{\_}*\langle{{x}.{\mathit{out}},{z}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*\mathit{reach}(z,y)*\mathsf{true}\end{array}

A
grid
is a connected network that, moreover, satisfies the
following formula:

\forall x\forall y\forall z~.~\langle{{x}.{\mathit{out}},{y}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*\langle{{x}.{\mathit{out}},{z}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*\mathsf{true}\rightarrow\exists u~.~\langle{{y}.{\mathit{out}},{u}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*\langle{{z}.{\mathit{out}},{u}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*\mathit{present}(x,y,z,u)

where

\mathit{present}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\mathop{\scalebox{1.9}{\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$\ast$}}}\hskip 1.0pt_{1\leq i\leq n}~{x_{i}}@{\_}
states that the components
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
are
present and pairwise distinct. Grids are important in modeling
distributed scientific computing
[
37
]
.

As suggested by work on Separation Logic

[
26
]
, the price to pay for this
expressivity is the inherent impossibility of having decision
procedures for a fragment of
CL
, that combines first-order
quantifiers with inductively defined predicates. A non-trivial
fragment of
CL
that has decision procedures for satisfiability and
entailment is the class of symbolic configurations

[
9
]
. However, we do not expect to describe
systems with clique or grid network topologies using symbolic
configurations. Furthermore, we conjecture that these classes are
beyond the expressiveness of the symbolic configuration fragment. We
discuss these issues in more detail in §
7
.

4
A Language for Programming Reconfigurations

This section defines
reconfiguration
actions that change the
structure of a configuration. We distinguish between reconfigurations
and havoc actions (Def.
3
), that change configurations
in orthogonal ways (see Fig.
2
for an
illustration of the interplay between the two types of actions). The
reconfiguration actions are the result of executing a given
reconfiguration program on the distributed system at hand. This
section presents the syntax and operational semantics of the
reconfiguration language. Later on, we introduce a Hoare-style proof
system to reason about the correctness of reconfiguration programs.

4.1
Syntax and Operational Semantics

Reconfiguration programs, ranged over by
\mathsf{R}
, are inductively
defined by the following syntax:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathsf{R}&::=&\mathsf{new}(q,x)\mid\mathsf{delete}(x)\mid\mathsf{connect}(x_{1}.p_{1},x_{2}.p_{2})\mid\mathsf{disconnect}(x_{1}.p_{1},x_{2}.p_{2})\\
&&\mid\mathsf{with}\;{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}}:{\theta}\;\mathsf{do}\;{\mathsf{R}_{1}}\;\mathsf{od}\mid\mathsf{R}_{1};\mathsf{R}_{2}\mid\mathsf{R}_{1}+\mathsf{R}_{2}\mid\mathsf{R}_{1}^{*}\end{array}

where
q\in Q
is a state,
x,x_{1},x_{2},\ldots\in\mathbb{V}
are
program variables and
\theta
is a
predicate-free

quantifier-free
formula of the
CL
logic, called a

trigger
.

The
primitive commands
are
\mathsf{new}(q,x)
and
\mathsf{delete}(x)
, that
create and delete a component (the newly created component is set to
execute from state
q
) given by the store value of
x
,

\mathsf{connect}(x_{1}.p_{1},x_{2}.p_{2})
and
\mathsf{disconnect}(x_{1}.p_{1},x_{2}.p_{2})
, that
create and delete an interaction, between the ports
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
of
the components given by the store values of
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
,
respectively. We denote by
\mathfrak{P}
the set of primitive commands.

A
conditional
is a program of the form
(\mathsf{with}\;{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}}:{\theta}\;\mathsf{do}\;{\mathsf{R}}\;\mathsf{od})
that performs the following steps,
with
no havoc action (Def.
3
) in between the first and second
steps
below:

1.

maps the variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
to some components
c_{1},\ldots,c_{k}\in\mathbb{C}
such that the configuration after the
assignment contains a model of the trigger
\theta
; the conditional
is disabled if the current configuration is not a model of
\exists x_{1}\ldots\exists x_{k}~.~\theta*\mathsf{true}
,

2.

launches the first command of the program
\mathsf{R}
on this
configuration, and

3.

continues with the remainder of
\mathsf{R}
, in interleaving with
havoc actions;

4.

upon completion of
\mathsf{R}
, the values of
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
are forgotten.

To avoid technical complications, we assume that nested conditionals
use pairwise disjoint tuples of variables; every program can be
statically changed to meet this condition, by renaming variables. Note
that the trigger
\theta
of a conditional
(\mathsf{with}\;{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}}:{\theta}\;\mathsf{do}\;{\mathsf{R}}\;\mathsf{od})
has no quantifiers nor predicate atoms, which
means that the overall number of components and interactions in a
model of
\theta
is polynomially bounded by the size of (number of
symbols needed to represent)
\theta
. Intuitively, this means that
the part of the system (matched by
\theta
) to which the
reconfiguration is applied is relatively small, thus the procedure
that evaluates the trigger can be easily implemented in a distributed
environment, as e.g., consensus between a small number of neighbouring
components.

The sequential composition
\mathsf{R}_{1};\mathsf{R}_{2}
executes
\mathsf{R}_{1}

followed by
\mathsf{R}_{2}
, with zero or more interactions firing in
between. This is because, even though being sequential, a
reconfiguration program runs in parallel with the state changes that
occur as a result of firing the interactions. Last,
\mathsf{R}_{1}+\mathsf{R}_{2}
executes either
\mathsf{R}_{1}
or
\mathsf{R}_{2}
, and
\mathsf{R}^{*}

executes
\mathsf{R}
zero or more times in sequence,
nondeterministically.

It is worth pointing out that the reconfiguration language does not
have explicit assignments between variables. As a matter of fact, the
conditionals are the only constructs that nondeterministically bind
variables to indices that satisfy a given logical constraint. This
design choice sustains the view of a distributed system as a cloud of
components and interactions in which reconfigurations can occur
anywhere a local condition is met. In other words, we do not need
variable assignments to traverse the architecture — the program
works rather by identifying a part of the system that matches a small
pattern, and applying the reconfiguration locally to that
subsystem. For instance, a typical pattern for writing reconfiguration
programs is
(\mathsf{with}\;{\mathbf{x}_{1}}:{\theta_{1}}\;\mathsf{do}\;{\mathsf{R}_{1}}\;\mathsf{od}+\ldots+\mathsf{with}\;{\mathbf{x}_{k}}:{\theta_{k}}\;\mathsf{do}\;{\mathsf{R}_{k}}\;\mathsf{od})^{*}
, where
\mathsf{R}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{R}_{k}
are loop-free sequential compositions of primitive
commands. This program continuously choses a reconfiguration sequence

\mathsf{R}_{i}
nondeterministically and either applies it on a small part
of the configuration that satisfies
\theta_{i}
, or does nothing, if no
such subconfiguration exists within the current configuration.

Figure 3
:
Operational Semantics of the Reconfiguration Language

c\in\mathbb{C}\!\setminus\!\mathcal{C}

{\mathsf{new}(q,x)}:{(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)}\leadsto{(\mathcal{C}\cup\{{c}\},\mathcal{I},\varrho[c\leftarrow q],\nu[x\leftarrow c])}

\nu(x)\in\mathcal{C}

{\mathsf{delete}(x)}:{(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)}\leadsto{(\mathcal{C}\!\setminus\!\{{\nu(x)}\},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)}

\nu(x)\not\in\mathcal{C}

{\mathsf{delete}(x)}:{(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)}~\text{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\leadsto$}}

{\mathsf{connect}(x_{1}.p_{1},x_{2}.p_{2})}:{(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)}\leadsto{(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I}\cup\{{(\nu(x_{1}),p_{1},\nu(x_{2}),p_{2})}\},\varrho,\nu)}

(\nu(x_{1}),p_{1},\nu(x_{2}),p_{2})\in\mathcal{I}

{\mathsf{disconnect}(x_{1}.p_{1},x_{2}.p_{2})}:{(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)}\leadsto{(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I}\!\setminus\!\{{(\nu(x_{1}),p_{1},\nu(x_{2}),p_{2})}\},\varrho,\nu)}

(\nu(x_{1}),p_{1},\nu(x_{2}),p_{2})\not\in\mathcal{I}

{\mathsf{disconnect}(x_{1}.p_{1},x_{2}.p_{2})}:{(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)}~\text{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\leadsto$}}

\begin{array}[]{l}c_{1},c^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,c_{k},c^{\prime}_{k}\in\mathbb{C}\hskip 11.38109pt(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu[x_{1}\leftarrow c_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\leftarrow c_{k}])\models\varphi*\mathsf{true}\\
{\mathsf{R}}:{(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu[x_{1}\leftarrow c_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\leftarrow c_{k}])}\leadsto{(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\varrho^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})}\end{array}

{\mathsf{with}\;{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}}:{\varphi}\;\mathsf{do}\;{\mathsf{R}}\;\mathsf{od}}:{(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)}\leadsto{(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\varrho^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}[x_{1}\leftarrow c^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\leftarrow c^{\prime}_{k}])}

\begin{array}[]{c}{\mathsf{R}_{1}}:{\gamma}\leadsto{\gamma_{0}}$ \hskip 8.50012pt$\gamma_{1}\in\mathfrak{h}(\gamma_{0})\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}{\mathsf{R}_{2}}:{\gamma_{1}}\leadsto{\gamma^{\prime}}\end{array}

{\mathsf{R}_{1};\mathsf{R}_{2}}:{\gamma}\leadsto{\gamma^{\prime}}

\begin{array}[]{c}{\mathsf{R}_{1}}:{\gamma}\leadsto{\gamma^{\prime}}\end{array}

{\mathsf{R}_{1}+\mathsf{R}_{2}}:{\gamma}\leadsto{\gamma^{\prime}}

{\mathsf{R}^{n}}:{\gamma}\leadsto{\gamma^{\prime}}

,
\mathsf{R}^{n}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathsf{R}^{n-1};\mathsf{R}&\text{if }n\geq 1\\
\mathsf{skip}&\text{if }n=0\end{array}\right.

{\mathsf{R}^{*}}:{\gamma}\leadsto{\gamma^{\prime}}

The operational semantics of reconfiguration programs is given by the
structural rules in Fig.
3
, that define the judgements

{\mathsf{R}}:{\gamma}\leadsto{\gamma^{\prime}}
and
{\mathsf{R}}:{\gamma}~\text{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\leadsto$}}
,
where
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
are configurations and
\mathsf{R}
is a
program. Intuitively,
{\mathsf{R}}:{\gamma}\leadsto{\gamma^{\prime}}
means that

\gamma^{\prime}
is a successor of
\gamma
following the execution of

\mathsf{R}
and
{\mathsf{R}}:{\gamma}~\text{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\leadsto$}}
means that
\mathsf{R}
faults in

\gamma
. The semantics of a program
\mathsf{R}
is the action

{\langle\!\langle}\mathsf{R}{\rangle\!\rangle}:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{pow}({\Gamma})^{\top}
, defined as :

{\langle\!\langle}\mathsf{R}{\rangle\!\rangle}(\gamma)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\top&\text{ if }{\mathsf{R}}:{\gamma}~\text{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\leadsto$}}\\
\{{\gamma^{\prime}\mid{\mathsf{R}}:{\gamma}\leadsto{\gamma^{\prime}}}\}&\text{ otherwise}\end{array}\right.

The only primitive commands that may fault are
\mathsf{delete}(x)
and

\mathsf{disconnect}(x_{1}.p_{1},x_{2}.p_{2})
; for both, the premisses of the faulty
rules are disjoint from the ones for normal termination, thus the
action
{\langle\!\langle}\mathsf{R}{\rangle\!\rangle}
is properly defined for all programs

\mathsf{R}
. Notice that the rule for sequential composition uses the
havoc action
\mathfrak{h}
in the premiss, thus capturing the interleaving
of havoc state changes and reconfiguration actions.

4.2
Reconfiguration Proof System

To reason about the correctness properties of reconfiguration
programs, we introduce a Hoare-style proof system consisting of a set
of axioms that formalize the primitive commands
(Fig.
4
a), a set of inference rules for the composite
programs (Fig.
4
b) and a set of structural rules
(Fig.
4
c). The judgements are Hoare triples

\{{\phi}\}~{\mathsf{R}}~\{{\psi}\}
, where
\phi
and
\psi
(called pre- and
postcondition, respectively) are
CL
formulæ. The triple

\{{\phi}\}~{\mathsf{R}}~\{{\psi}\}
is
valid
, written
\models\{{\phi}\}~{\mathsf{R}}~\{{\psi}\}
, if and only if

{\langle\!\langle}\mathsf{R}{\rangle\!\rangle}({{\bf[\![}\phi{\bf]\!]}})\subseteq{{\bf[\![}\psi{\bf]\!]}}
. Note that a
triple is valid only if the program does not fault on any model of the
precondition. In other words, an invalid Hoare triple

\{{\phi}\}~{\mathsf{R}}~\{{\psi}\}
cannot distinguish between

{\langle\!\langle}\mathsf{R}{\rangle\!\rangle}({{\bf[\![}\phi{\bf]\!]}})\not\subseteq{{\bf[\![}\psi{\bf]\!]}}

(non-faulting incorrectness) and
{\langle\!\langle}\mathsf{R}{\rangle\!\rangle}({{\bf[\![}\phi{\bf]\!]}})=\top
(faulting).

The axioms (Fig.
4
a) give the
local
specifications
of the primitive commands in the language by Hoare
triples whose preconditions describe only those resources (components
and interactions) necessary to avoid faulting. In particular,

\mathsf{delete}(x)
and
\mathsf{disconnect}(x_{1}.p_{1},x_{2}.p_{2})
require a single
component
{x}@{\_}
and an interaction

\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle
to avoid faulting, respectively. The
rules for sequential composition and iteration (Fig
4
b)
use the following semantic side condition, based on the havoc action
(Def.
3
):

Definition 4

A formula
\phi
is
havoc invariant
if and only if

\mathfrak{h}({{\bf[\![}\phi{\bf]\!]}})\subseteq{{\bf[\![}\phi{\bf]\!]}}
.

Note that the dual inclusion
{{\bf[\![}\phi{\bf]\!]}}\subseteq\mathfrak{h}({{\bf[\![}\phi{\bf]\!]}})
always holds, because
\mathfrak{h}
is the
reflexive and transitive closure of the
~\xRightarrow{{\scriptstyle}}_{\raisebox{4.0pt}{\!$\scriptstyle{}$}}~
relation
(Def.
3
). Since havoc invariance is required to prove
the validity of Hoare triples involving sequential composition, it is
important to have a way of checking havoc invariance. We describe a
proof system for such havoc queries in §
5
. Moreover,
the side condition of the
consequence rule

(Fig.
4
c left) consists of two entailments, that are
discharged by an external decision procedure (discussed in
§
7
).

Figure 4
:
Proof System for the Reconfiguration Language

\{{\mathsf{emp}}\}~{\mathsf{new}(q,x)}~\{{{x}@{q}}\}

\{{{x}@{\_}}\}~{\mathsf{delete}(x)}~\{{\mathsf{emp}}\}

\{{\mathsf{emp}}\}~{\mathsf{connect}(x_{1}.p_{1},x_{2}.p_{2})}~\{{\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle}\}

\{{\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle}\}~{\mathsf{disconnect}(x_{1}.p_{1},x_{2}.p_{2})}~\{{\mathsf{emp}}\}

a. Axioms for Primitive Commands

\{{\phi\wedge(\theta*\mathsf{true})}\}~{\mathsf{R}}~\{{\psi}\}

\mathrm{fv}({\phi})\cap\{{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}}\}=\emptyset

\{{\phi}\}~{\mathsf{with}\;{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}}:{\theta}\;\mathsf{do}\;{\mathsf{R}}\;\mathsf{od}}~\{{\exists x_{1}\ldots\exists x_{k}~.~\psi}\}

\{{\phi}\}~{\mathsf{R}_{1}}~\{{\varphi}\}

\{{\varphi}\}~{\mathsf{R}_{2}}~\{{\psi}\}

\mathfrak{h}({{\bf[\![}\varphi{\bf]\!]}})\subseteq{{\bf[\![}\varphi{\bf]\!]}}

\{{\phi}\}~{\mathsf{R}_{1};\mathsf{R}_{2}}~\{{\psi}\}

\{{\phi}\}~{\mathsf{R}_{1}}~\{{\psi}\}

\{{\phi}\}~{\mathsf{R}_{2}}~\{{\psi}\}

\{{\phi}\}~{\mathsf{R}_{1}+\mathsf{R}_{2}}~\{{\psi}\}

\{{\phi}\}~{\mathsf{R}}~\{{\phi}\}

\mathfrak{h}({{\bf[\![}\phi{\bf]\!]}})\subseteq{{\bf[\![}\phi{\bf]\!]}}

\{{\phi}\}~{\mathsf{R}^{*}}~\{{\phi}\}

b. Inference Rules for Programs

\{{\phi_{i}}\}~{\mathsf{R}}~\{{\psi_{i}}\}\mid i\in[{1},{k}]

\{{\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}\phi_{i}}\}~{\mathsf{R}}~\{{\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}\psi_{i}}\}

\{{\phi_{i}}\}~{\mathsf{R}}~\{{\psi_{i}}\}\mid i\in[{1},{k}]

\{{\bigwedge_{i=1}^{k}\phi_{i}}\}~{\mathsf{R}}~\{{\bigwedge_{i=1}^{k}\psi_{i}}\}

\{{\phi^{\prime}}\}~{\mathsf{R}}~\{{\psi^{\prime}}\}

\begin{array}[]{l}\phi\models\phi^{\prime}\\
\psi^{\prime}\models\psi\end{array}

\{{\phi}\}~{\mathsf{R}}~\{{\psi}\}

\{{\phi}\}~{\mathsf{R}}~\{{\psi}\}

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{R}\in\mathfrak{L}\\
\mathsf{modif}({\mathsf{R}})\cap\mathrm{fv}({F})=\emptyset\end{array}

\{{\phi*F}\}~{\mathsf{R}}~\{{\psi*F}\}

c. Structural Inference Rules

The
frame rule
(Fig.
4
c bottom-right) allows to
apply the specification of a
local program
, defined below, to a
set of configurations that may contain more resources (components and
interactions) than the ones asserted by the precondition. Intuitively,
a local program requires a bounded amount of components and
interactions to avoid faulting and, moreover, it only changes the
configuration of the local subsystem, not affecting the entire
system’s configuration. Formally, the set
\mathfrak{L}
of local
programs is the least set that contains the primitive commands

\mathfrak{P}
and is closed under the application of the following
rules:

\mathsf{R}\in\mathfrak{L}\Rightarrow\mathsf{with}\;{\mathbf{x}}:{\pi}\;\mathsf{do}\;{\mathsf{R}}\;\mathsf{od}\in\mathfrak{L}\text{, if $\pi$ is a conjunction of (dis-)equalities}\hskip 11.38109pt\mathsf{R}_{1},\mathsf{R}_{2}\in\mathfrak{L}\Rightarrow\mathsf{R}_{1}+\mathsf{R}_{2}\in\mathfrak{L}

The extra resources, not required to execute a local program, are
specified by a frame
F
, whose free variables are not modified by the
local program
\mathsf{R}
. Formally, the set of variables modified by a
local program
\mathsf{R}\in\mathfrak{L}
is defined inductively on its
structure:

\begin{array}[]{l}\hskip-5.69054pt\mathsf{modif}({\mathsf{new}(q,x)})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\{{x}\}\hskip 71.13188pt\mathsf{modif}({\mathsf{R}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\emptyset\text{, for all }\mathsf{R}\in\mathfrak{P}\!\setminus\!\{{\mathsf{new}(q,x)\mid q\in Q,x\in\mathbb{V}}\}\\
\hskip-5.69054pt\mathsf{modif}({\mathsf{with}\;{\mathbf{x}}:{\theta}\;\mathsf{do}\;{\mathsf{R}}\;\mathsf{od}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\mathbf{x}\cup\mathsf{modif}({\mathsf{R}})\hskip 51.21504pt\mathsf{modif}({\mathsf{R}_{1}+\mathsf{R}_{2}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\mathsf{modif}({\mathsf{R}_{1}})\cup\mathsf{modif}({\mathsf{R}_{2}})\end{array}

The frame rule is sound only for programs whose semantics are

local actions
, defined below:

Definition 5
(Locality)

Given a set of variables
X\subseteq\mathbb{V}
, an action
f:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{pow}({\Gamma})^{\top}
is
local
for
X
if and only if
f(\gamma_{1}\bullet\gamma_{2})\subseteq f(\gamma_{1})\bullet{\{{\gamma_{2}}\}}\!\!\uparrow^{\scriptscriptstyle{X}}
for all
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in\Gamma
, where, for any set
S
of
configurations:

{S}\!\!\uparrow^{\scriptscriptstyle{X}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\left\{{(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})\mid(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)\in S,\forall x\in\mathbb{V}\!\setminus\!X~.~\nu^{\prime}(x)=\nu(x),~\forall c\in\mathbb{C}\!\setminus\!\nu(X)~.~\varrho^{\prime}(c)=\varrho(c)}\right\}.

An action
f
is
local
if and only if
it is local for the empty set of variables.

An action that is local for a set of variables
X
allows for the
change of the store values of the variables in
X
and the states of
the components indexed by those values, only. Essentially,
\mathsf{new}(q,x)

is local for
\{{x}\}
, because the fresh index associated to
x
is
nondeterministically chosen and the state is
q
, whereas the other
primitive commands are local, in general. The semantics of every local
program
\mathsf{R}\in\mathfrak{L}
is a local action, as shown below:

Lemma 1

For every program
\mathsf{R}\in\mathfrak{L}
, the action

{\langle\!\langle}\mathsf{R}{\rangle\!\rangle}
is local for
\mathsf{modif}({\mathsf{R}})
.

Moreover,
\mathfrak{L}
is precisely the set of programs with local
semantics, as conditionals and sequential compositions (hence also
iterations) are not local, in general:

Example 5

To understand why
\mathfrak{L}
is precisely the set of local
commands, consider the programs:

•

(\mathsf{with}\;{x}:{{x}@{q}}\;\mathsf{do}\;{\mathsf{delete}(x)}\;\mathsf{od})
is
not local because, letting
\gamma_{1}
be a configuration with
zero components and
\gamma_{2}
be a configuration with one
component in state
q
, we have:

\begin{array}[]{l}{\langle\!\langle}\mathsf{with}\;{x}:{{x}@{q}}\;\mathsf{do}\;{\mathsf{delete}(x)}\;\mathsf{od}{\rangle\!\rangle}(\gamma_{1}\bullet\gamma_{2})={\langle\!\langle}\mathsf{with}\;{x}:{{x}@{q}}\;\mathsf{do}\;{\mathsf{delete}(x)}\;\mathsf{od}{\rangle\!\rangle}(\gamma_{2})=\{{\gamma_{1}}\}\\
\text{whereas }{\langle\!\langle}\mathsf{with}\;{x}:{{x}@{q}}\;\mathsf{do}\;{\mathsf{delete}(x)}\;\mathsf{od}{\rangle\!\rangle}(\gamma_{1})\bullet\{{\gamma_{2}}\}=\emptyset\bullet\{{\gamma_{2}}\}=\emptyset.\end{array}

•

(\mathsf{skip};\mathsf{skip})
is not local because, considering
the system from Fig.
1
, if we take
\gamma_{1}
and

\gamma_{2}
, such that
\gamma_{1}\models{x}@{\mathsf{T}}
and
\gamma_{2}\models\langle{{x}.{\mathit{out}},{y}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*{y}@{\mathsf{H}}
, we have:

\begin{array}[]{l}{\langle\!\langle}\mathsf{skip};\mathsf{skip}{\rangle\!\rangle}(\gamma_{1}\bullet\gamma_{2})={{\bf[\![}{x}@{\mathsf{T}}*\langle{{x}.{\mathit{out}},{y}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*{y}@{\mathsf{H}}{\bf]\!]}}~\cup~{{\bf[\![}{x}@{\mathsf{H}}*\langle{{x}.{\mathit{out}},{y}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*{y}@{\mathsf{T}}{\bf]\!]}}\\
\text{whereas }{\langle\!\langle}\mathsf{skip};\mathsf{skip}{\rangle\!\rangle}(\gamma_{1})\bullet\{{\gamma_{2}}\}={{\bf[\![}{x}@{\mathsf{T}}*\langle{{x}.{\mathit{out}},{y}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*{y}@{\mathsf{H}}{\bf]\!]}}\hfill\blacksquare\end{array}

We write
\vdash\{{\phi}\}~{\mathsf{R}}~\{{\psi}\}
if and only if

\{{\phi}\}~{\mathsf{R}}~\{{\psi}\}
can be derived from the axioms using the
inference rules from Fig.
4
and show the soundness of
the proof system in the following. The next lemma gives sufficient
conditions for the soundness of the axioms (Fig.
4
a):

Lemma 2

For each axiom
\{{\phi}\}~{\mathsf{R}}~\{{\psi}\}
, where
\mathsf{R}\in\mathfrak{P}
is primitive, we have
{\langle\!\langle}\mathsf{R}{\rangle\!\rangle}({{\bf[\![}\phi{\bf]\!]}})={{\bf[\![}\psi{\bf]\!]}}
.

The soundness of the proof system in Fig.
4
follows from
the soundness of each inference rule:

Theorem 1

For any Hoare triple
\{{\phi}\}~{\mathsf{R}}~\{{\psi}\}
, if
\vdash\{{\phi}\}~{\mathsf{R}}~\{{\psi}\}
then
\models\{{\phi}\}~{\mathsf{R}}~\{{\psi}\}
.

As an optimization, reconfiguration proofs can often be simplified, by
safely skipping the check of one or more havoc invariance side
conditions of sequential compositions, as explained below.

Definition 6

A program of the form
\mathsf{disconnect}(x_{1}.p_{1},x^{\prime}_{1}.p^{\prime}_{1});~\ldots~\mathsf{disconnect}(x_{k}.p_{k},x^{\prime}_{k}.p^{\prime}_{k});

\mathsf{connect}(x_{k+1}.p_{k+1},x^{\prime}_{k+1}.p^{\prime}_{k+1});~\ldots~\mathsf{connect}(x_{\ell}.p_{\ell},x^{\prime}_{\ell}.p^{\prime}_{\ell})
is said to be a
single reversal program
.

Single reversal programs first disconnect components and then
reconnect them in a different way. For such programs, only the first
and last application of the sequential composition rule require
checking havoc invariance:

Proposition 1

Let
\mathsf{R}=\mathsf{disconnect}(x_{1}.p_{1},x^{\prime}_{1}.p^{\prime}_{1});~\ldots~\mathsf{disconnect}(x_{k}.p_{k},x^{\prime}_{k}.p^{\prime}_{k});

\mathsf{connect}(x_{k+1}.p_{k+1},x^{\prime}_{k+1}.p^{\prime}_{k+1});~\ldots~\mathsf{connect}(x_{\ell}.p_{\ell},x^{\prime}_{\ell}.p^{\prime}_{\ell})
be a single reversal
program. If
\phi_{0},\ldots,\phi_{\ell}
are
CL
formulæ, such
that:

•

\models\{{\phi_{i-1}}\}~{\mathsf{disconnect}(x_{i}.p_{i},x^{\prime}_{i}.p^{\prime}_{i})}~\{{\phi_{i}}\}
, for all
i\in[{1},{k}]
,

•

\models\{{\phi_{j-1}}\}~{\mathsf{connect}(x_{j}.p_{j},x^{\prime}_{j}.p^{\prime}_{j})}~\{{\phi_{j}}\}
, for
all
j\in[{k+1},{\ell}]
, and

•

\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{\ell-1}
are havoc invariant,

then we have
\models\{{\phi_{0}}\}~{\mathsf{R}}~\{{\phi_{\ell}}\}
.

4.3
Examples of Reconfiguration Proofs

We prove that the outcome of the reconfiguration program from
Fig.
1
(Listing
2
),
started in a token ring configuration with at least two components in
state
\mathsf{H}
and at least one in state
\mathsf{T}
, is a token
ring with at least one component in each state. The pre- and
postcondition are
\exists x\exists y~.~\mathsf{chain}_{{2},{1}}(x,y)*\langle{{y}.{\mathit{out}},{x}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle
and
\exists x\exists y~.~\mathsf{chain}_{{1},{1}}(x,y)*\langle{{y}.{\mathit{out}},{x}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle
, respectively, with the
definitions of
\mathsf{chain}_{{h},{t}}(x,y)
given in Example
4
,
for all constants
h,t\in\mathbb{N}
.

⬇

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\{{\exists x\exists y~.~\mathsf{chain}_{{2},{1}}(x,y)*\langle{{y}.{\mathit{out}},{x}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle}\}}

with

x,y,z:\langle{{x}.{\mathit{out}},{y}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle\ast{y}@{\mathsf{H}}\ast\langle{{y}.{\mathit{out}},{z}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle

do

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\{{(\exists x\exists y~.~\mathsf{chain}_{{2},{1}}(x,y)*\langle{{y}.{\mathit{out}},{x}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle)\wedge\big(\langle{{x}.{\mathit{out}},{y}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*{y}@{\mathsf{H}}*\langle{{y}.{\mathit{out}},{z}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*\mathsf{true}\big)}\}}

(\star)

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\{{\langle{{x}.{\mathit{out}},{y}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*\ovalbox{${{y}@{\mathsf{H}}*\langle{{y}.{\mathit{out}},{z}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*\mathsf{chain}_{{1},{1}}(z,x)}$}}\}}

disconnect
(
x
.
\mathit{out}
,

y
.
\mathit{in}
);

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\{{\ovalbox{${{y}@{\mathsf{H}}}$}*\langle{{y}.{\mathit{out}},{z}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*\ovalbox{${\mathsf{chain}_{{1},{1}}(z,x)}$}}\}}

(\sharp)

disconnect
(
y
.
\mathit{out}
,

z
.
\mathit{in}
);

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\{{{y}@{\mathsf{H}}*\ovalbox{${\mathsf{chain}_{{1},{1}}(z,x)}$}}\}}

(\sharp)

⬇

delete
(
y
);

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\{{\ovalbox{${\mathsf{chain}_{{1},{1}}(z,x)}$}}\}}

(\sharp)

connect
(
x
.
\mathit{out}
,

z
.
\mathit{in}
)

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\{{\mathsf{chain}_{{1},{1}}(z,x)*\langle{{x}.{\mathit{out}},{z}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle}\}}

od

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\{{\exists x\exists y~.~\mathsf{chain}_{{1},{1}}(x,y)*\langle{{y}.{\mathit{out}},{x}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle}\}}

The inference rule for conditional programs sets up the precondition

(\star)
for the body of the conditional. This formula is equivalent
to
\langle{{x}.{\mathit{out}},{y}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*{y}@{\mathsf{H}}*\langle{{y}.{\mathit{out}},{z}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*\mathsf{chain}_{{1},{1}}(z,x)
. To understand this
point, we derive from
(\star)
the equivalences:

\begin{array}[]{l}(\exists x\exists y~.~\mathsf{chain}_{{2},{1}}(x,y)*\langle{{y}.{\mathit{out}},{x}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle)\wedge(\langle{{x}.{\mathit{out}},{y}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*{y}@{\mathsf{H}}*\langle{{y}.{\mathit{out}},{z}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*\mathsf{true})\equiv\\
\exists\overline{x}\exists\overline{y}\exists\overline{z}~.~\langle{{\overline{x}}.{\mathit{out}},{\overline{y}}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*{\overline{y}}@{\mathsf{H}}*\langle{{\overline{y}}.{\mathit{out}},{\overline{z}}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*\mathsf{chain}_{{1},{1}}(\overline{z},\overline{x})\wedge(\langle{{x}.{\mathit{out}},{y}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*{y}@{\mathsf{H}}*\langle{{y}.{\mathit{out}},{z}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*\mathsf{true})\\
\equiv\langle{{x}.{\mathit{out}},{y}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*{y}@{\mathsf{H}}*\langle{{y}.{\mathit{out}},{z}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*\mathsf{chain}_{{1},{1}}(z,x)\end{array}

where the first step can be proven by entailment checking (discussed
in §
7
). The following four annotations above
are obtained by applications of the axioms and the frame rule (the
frame formulæ are displayed within boxes). The sequential
composition rule is applied by proving first that the annotations
marked as
(\sharp)
are havoc invariant (the proof is given later in
§
5.4
).

We have considered the reconfiguration program from
Fig.
1
(Listing
2
)
which deletes a component from a token ring. The dual operation is the
addition of a new component. Here the precondition states that the
system is a valid token ring, with at least one component in state

\mathsf{H}
and at least another one in state
\mathsf{T}
. We prove
that the execution of the dual program yields a token ring with at least
two components in state
\mathsf{H}
, as the new component is added
without a token.

⬇

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\{{\exists x\exists y~.~\mathsf{chain}_{{1},{1}}(x,y)*\langle{{y}.{\mathit{out}},{x}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle}\}}

with

x,z:\langle{{x}.{\mathit{out}},{z}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle

do

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\{{(\exists x\exists y~.~\mathsf{chain}_{{1},{1}}(x,y)*\langle{{y}.{\mathit{out}},{x}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle)\wedge\big(\langle{{x}.{\mathit{out}},{z}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*\mathsf{true}\big)}\}}

(\star)

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\{{\langle{{x}.{\mathit{out}},{z}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*\ovalbox{${\mathsf{chain}_{{1},{1}}(z,x)}$}}\}}

disconnect
(
x
.
\mathit{out}
,

z
.
\mathit{in}
);

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\{{\mathsf{chain}_{{1},{1}}(z,x)}\}}

(\sharp)

new
(
\mathsf{H}
,
y
);

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\{{{y}@{\mathsf{H}}*\ovalbox{${\mathsf{chain}_{{1},{1}}(z,x)}$}}\}}

(\sharp)

⬇

connect
(
y
.
\mathit{out}
,
z
.
\mathit{in}
);

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\{{\ovalbox{${\scriptstyle{y}@{\mathsf{H}}}$}*\langle{{y}.{\mathit{out}},{z}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*\ovalbox{${\mathsf{chain}_{{1},{1}}(z,x)}$}}\}}

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\{{\mathsf{chain}_{{2},{1}}(y,x)}\}}

(\sharp)

connect
(
x
.
\mathit{out}
,
y
.
\mathit{in}
)

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\{{\mathsf{chain}_{{2},{1}}(y,x)*\langle{{x}.{\mathit{out}},{y}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle}\}}

od

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\{{\exists x\exists y~.~\mathsf{chain}_{{2},{1}}(x,y)*\langle{{y}.{\mathit{out}},{x}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle}\}}

The annotation
(\star)
is given by the inference rule for
conditional programs. Then we can derive the equivalence
(\exists x\exists y~.~\mathsf{chain}_{{1},{1}}(x,y)*\langle{{y}.{\mathit{out}},{x}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle)\wedge\big(\langle{{x}.{\mathit{out}},{z}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*\mathsf{true}\big)\equiv\langle{{x}.{\mathit{out}},{z}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*\mathsf{chain}_{{1},{1}}(z,x)
. In the subsequent
lines, some axioms and the frame rule are applied (the frame is
displayed in the postconditions of the commands within the boxes). The
annotations marked as
(\sharp)
must be shown to be havoc invariant and
then the sequential composition rule is applied to complete the proof.

5
The Havoc Proof System

This section describes a set of axioms and inference rules for proving
the validity of havoc invariance queries of the form

\mathfrak{h}({{\bf[\![}\phi{\bf]\!]}})\subseteq{{\bf[\![}\phi{\bf]\!]}}
, where
\phi
is a

CL
formula interpreted over a given SID and
\mathfrak{h}
is the havoc
action (Def.
3
). Such a query is valid if and only if
the result of applying any sequence of interactions on a model of

\phi
is again a model of
\phi
(Def.
4
).
Havoc invariance queries occur as side conditions in the rules for
sequential composition and iteration (Fig.
4
b) of
reconfiguration programs. Thus, having a proof system for havoc
invariance is crucial for the applicability of the rules in
Fig.
4
to obtain proofs of reconfiguration programs.

The havoc proof system uses a compositional rule, able to split a
query of the form
\mathfrak{h}({{\bf[\![}\phi_{1}*\phi_{2}{\bf]\!]}})\subseteq{{\bf[\![}\psi_{1}*\psi_{2}{\bf]\!]}}
into two queries
\mathfrak{h}({{\bf[\![}\phi_{i}*\mathcal{F}_{i}{\bf]\!]}})\subseteq{{\bf[\![}\psi_{i}*\mathcal{F}_{i}{\bf]\!]}}
, where
each
frontier formula

\mathcal{F}_{i}
defines a set of
interactions that over-approximate the effect of executing the system
described by
\phi_{3-i}
(resp.
\psi_{3-i}
) over the one described
by
\phi_{i}
(resp.
\psi_{i}
), for
i=1,2
. In principle, the
frontier formulæ (
\mathcal{F}_{1}
and
\mathcal{F}_{2}
) can be
understood as describing the interference between parallel actions in
an assume/rely guarantee-style parallel composition rule

[
58
,
42
]
. In particular, since the
frontier formulæ only describe interactions and carry no state
information whatsoever, such assumptions about events triggered by the
environment are reminiscent of compositional reasoning about
input/output automata
[
23
]
.

Compositional reasoning about havoc actions requires the following
relaxation of the definition of havoc state changes
(Def.
3
), by allowing the firing of loose, in addition
to tight interactions:

Definition 7

The following rules define a relation
\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle(c_{1},p_{1},c_{2},p_{2})}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle(c_{1},p_{1},c_{2},p_{2})}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle(c_{1},p_{1},c_{2},p_{2})}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle(c_{1},p_{1},c_{2},p_{2})}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}_{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\!$\scriptstyle{}$}}~\subseteq\Gamma\times\Gamma
, parameterized by
a given interaction
(c_{1},p_{1},c_{2},p_{2})
:

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}(c_{1},p_{1},c_{2},p_{2})\in\mathcal{I}&c_{i}\in\mathcal{C},c_{3-i}\not\in\mathcal{C}&\varrho(c_{i})=q_{i}&q_{i}\xrightarrow{{\scriptstyle p_{i}}}q^{\prime}_{i}\end{array}

(
\mathsf{Loose}
)

i=1,2

(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle(c_{1},p_{1},c_{2},p_{2})}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle(c_{1},p_{1},c_{2},p_{2})}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle(c_{1},p_{1},c_{2},p_{2})}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle(c_{1},p_{1},c_{2},p_{2})}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}_{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\!$\scriptstyle{}$}}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho[c_{i}\leftarrow q^{\prime}_{i}],\nu)

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}(c_{1},p_{1},c_{2},p_{2})\in\mathcal{I}&c_{1}\neq c_{2}\in\mathcal{C}&\varrho(c_{i})=q_{i}&q_{i}\xrightarrow{{\scriptstyle p_{i}}}q^{\prime}_{i},~i=1,2\end{array}

(
\mathsf{Tight}
)

(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle(c_{1},p_{1},c_{2},p_{2})}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle(c_{1},p_{1},c_{2},p_{2})}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle(c_{1},p_{1},c_{2},p_{2})}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle(c_{1},p_{1},c_{2},p_{2})}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}_{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\!$\scriptstyle{}$}}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho[c_{1}\leftarrow q^{\prime}_{1}][c_{2}\leftarrow q^{\prime}_{2}],\nu)

For a sequence
w=i_{1}\ldots i_{n}
of interactions, we define

\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle w}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle w}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle w}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle w}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}_{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\!$\scriptstyle{}$}}
to be the composition of
\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle i_{1}}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle i_{1}}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle i_{1}}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle i_{1}}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}_{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\!$\scriptstyle{}$}},\ldots,\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle i_{n}}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle i_{n}}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle i_{n}}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle i_{n}}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}_{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\!$\scriptstyle{}$}}
, assumed to be the identity relation, if
w
is empty.

The difference with Def.
3
is that only the states of
the components from the configuration are changed according to the
transitions in the behavior. This more relaxed definition matches the
intuition of partial systems in which certain interactions may be
controlled by an external environment; those interactions are
conservatively assumed to fire anytime they are enabled by the
components of the current structure, independently of the environment.

Example 6

(contd. from Example
3
)
Let
\widetilde{\gamma}_{i}=(\{{c_{2},c_{3}}\},\{{(c_{i},\mathit{out},c_{i~\mathrm{mod}~3+1},\mathit{in})\mid i\in[{1},{3}]}\},\varrho_{i},\nu)
, for
i\in[{1},{3}]
be the top-most
configurations from Fig.
2
without the

c_{1}
component, where
\varrho_{1}(c_{2})=\mathsf{H}
,

\varrho_{1}(c_{3})=\mathsf{T}
,
\varrho_{2}(c_{2})=\varrho_{2}(c_{3})=\mathsf{H}
,
\varrho_{3}(c_{2})=\mathsf{T}
,

\varrho_{3}(c_{3})=\mathsf{H}
. Then, by executing the loose
interactions
(c_{3},\mathit{out},c_{1},\mathit{in})
and

(c_{1},\mathit{out},c_{2},\mathit{in})
from
\gamma_{1}
, we obtain:

\widetilde{\gamma}_{1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle(c_{3},\mathit{out},c_{1},\mathit{in})}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle(c_{3},\mathit{out},c_{1},\mathit{in})}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle(c_{3},\mathit{out},c_{1},\mathit{in})}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle(c_{3},\mathit{out},c_{1},\mathit{in})}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}_{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\!$\scriptstyle{}$}}\widetilde{\gamma}_{2}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle(c_{1},\mathit{out},c_{2},\mathit{in})}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle(c_{1},\mathit{out},c_{2},\mathit{in})}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle(c_{1},\mathit{out},c_{2},\mathit{in})}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle(c_{1},\mathit{out},c_{2},\mathit{in})}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}_{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\!$\scriptstyle{}$}}\widetilde{\gamma}_{3}

Executing the tight interaction
(c_{2},\mathit{out},c_{3},\mathit{in})
from
\widetilde{\gamma}_{3}
leads back to
\widetilde{\gamma}_{1}
i.e.,

\widetilde{\gamma}_{3}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle(c_{2},\mathit{out},c_{3},\mathit{in})}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle(c_{2},\mathit{out},c_{3},\mathit{in})}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle(c_{2},\mathit{out},c_{3},\mathit{in})}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle(c_{2},\mathit{out},c_{3},\mathit{in})}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}_{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\!$\scriptstyle{}$}}\widetilde{\gamma}_{1}
.
\blacksquare

5.1
Regular Expressions

Proving the validity of a havoc query
\mathfrak{h}({{\bf[\![}\phi{\bf]\!]}})\subseteq{{\bf[\![}\psi{\bf]\!]}}
involves reasoning about the sequences of interactions
that define the outcome of the havoc action. We specify languages of
such sequences using extended regular expressions, defined inductively
by the following syntax:

\mathsf{L}::=\epsilon\mid\Sigma[{\alpha}]\mid\mathsf{L}\cdot\mathsf{L}\mid\mathsf{L}\cup\mathsf{L}\mid\mathsf{L}^{*}\mid\mathsf{L}\bowtie_{\eta,\eta}\mathsf{L}

where
\epsilon
denotes the empty
string,
\Sigma[{\alpha}]
is an
alphabet symbol
associated
with either an interaction atom or a predicate atom
\alpha
and

\cdot
,
\cup
and
{~}{}^{*}
are the usual concatenation, union and
Kleene star. By
\mathsf{L}_{1}\bowtie_{\eta_{1},\eta_{2}}\mathsf{L}_{2}
we denote
the interleaving (zip) product of the languages described by
\mathsf{L}_{1}

and
\mathsf{L}_{2}
with respect to the sets
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
of
alphabet symbols of the form
\Sigma[{\alpha}]
, respectively.

The
language
of a regular expression
\mathsf{L}
in a configuration

\gamma=(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)
is defined below:

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\langle\!\!\langle}{\epsilon}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\{{\epsilon}\}&{\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\alpha}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\bigcup\{{\mathcal{I}\mid(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)\sqsubseteq\gamma,~(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)\models\alpha}\}\\
{\langle\!\!\langle}{\mathsf{L}_{1}\cdot\mathsf{L}_{2}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\{{w_{1}w_{2}\mid w_{i}\in{\langle\!\!\langle}{\mathsf{L}_{i}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma}),~i=1,2}\}&{\langle\!\!\langle}{\mathsf{L}_{1}\cup\mathsf{L}_{2}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}{\langle\!\!\langle}{\mathsf{L}_{1}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})\cup{\langle\!\!\langle}{\mathsf{L}_{2}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})\\
{\langle\!\!\langle}{\mathsf{L}^{*}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\bigcup_{i\geq 0}{\langle\!\!\langle}{\mathsf{L}^{i}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})&{\langle\!\!\langle}{\mathsf{L}_{1}\bowtie_{\eta_{1},\eta_{2}}\mathsf{L}_{2}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\{{w\mid{w}\!\downarrow_{\scriptscriptstyle{{\langle\!\!\langle}{\eta_{i}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})}}\in{\langle\!\!\langle}{\mathsf{L}_{i}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma}),~i=1,2}\}\end{array}

where
{\langle\!\!\langle}{\eta}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\bigcup_{\Sigma[{\alpha}]\in\eta}{\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\alpha}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})
and

{w}\!\downarrow_{\scriptscriptstyle{{\langle\!\!\langle}{\eta}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})}}
is the word obtained from
w
by
deleting each symbol not in
{\langle\!\!\langle}{\eta}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})
from it. The

i
-th composition of
\mathsf{L}
with itself is defined, as usual, by

\mathsf{L}^{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\epsilon
and
\mathsf{L}^{i+1}=\mathsf{L}^{i}\cdot\mathsf{L}
, for
i\geq 0
. We denote by
\mathrm{supp}({\mathsf{L}})
the
support
of
\mathsf{L}
i.e.,
set of alphabet symbols
\Sigma[{\alpha}]
from the regular expression

\mathsf{L}
.

Example 7

Let
\gamma=(\{{c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},c_{4}}\},\{{(c_{1},\mathit{out},c_{2},\mathit{in}),(c_{2},\mathit{out},c_{3},\mathit{in}),(c_{3},\mathit{out},c_{4},\mathit{in})}\},\varrho,\nu)
be a
configuration, such that
\nu(x)=c_{1}
,
\nu(y)=c_{2}
and

\nu(z)=c_{3}
. Then, we have

{\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\langle{{x}.{\mathit{out}},{y}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})=\{{(c_{1},\mathit{out},c_{2},\mathit{in})}\}
,

{\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\langle{{y}.{\mathit{out}},{z}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})=\{{(c_{2},\mathit{out},c_{3},\mathit{in})}\}
and

{\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\mathsf{chain}_{{0},{0}}(x,z)}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})=\{(c_{1},\mathit{out},c_{2},\mathit{in})
,
(c_{2},\mathit{out},c_{3},\mathit{in})\}
.
\blacksquare

Given a configuration
\gamma
and a predicate atom
\alpha
, there
can be, in principle, more than one subconfiguration
\gamma^{\prime}\sqsubseteq\gamma
, such that
\gamma^{\prime}\models\alpha
. This is
problematic, because then
{\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\alpha}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})
may
contain interactions from different subconfigurations of
\gamma
,
that are models of
\alpha
, thus cluttering the definition of the
language
{\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\alpha}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})
. We fix this issue by
adapting the notion of
precision
, originally introduced for

SL

[
18
,
57
]
, to our
configuration logic:

Definition 8
(Precision)

A formula
\phi
is
precise on a set
S
of configurations
if
and only if, for every configuration
\gamma\in S
, there exists
at most one configuration
\gamma^{\prime}
, such that
\gamma^{\prime}\sqsubseteq\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}\models\phi
. A set of
formulæ
\Phi
is
precisely closed
if
\psi
is precise
on
{{\bf[\![}\phi{\bf]\!]}}
, for any two formulæ
\phi,\psi\in\Phi
.

Symbolic configurations using predicate atoms are not precise for

\Gamma
, in general
5
5

5

Unlike the predicates that define
acyclic data structures (lists, trees) in
SL
, which are
typically precise.
. To understand this point, consider a
configuration consisting of two overlapping models of

\mathsf{chain}_{{h},{t}}(x,y)
, starting and ending in
x
and
y
, respectively,
with a component that branches on two interactions after
x
and
another component that joins the two branches before
y
. Then

\mathsf{chain}_{{h},{t}}(x,y)
is not precise on such configurations (that are
not models of
\mathsf{chain}_{{h},{t}}(x,y)
whatsoever). On the positive side,
we can state the following:

Proposition 2

The set of symbolic configurations built using predicate atoms

\mathsf{chain}_{{h},{t}}(x,y)
, for
h,t\geq 0
(Example
4
) is
precisely closed.

Two regular expressions are
congruent
if they denote the same
language, whenever interpreted in the same configuration. Lifted to
models of a symbolic configuration, we define:

Definition 9

Given a symbolic configuration
\phi
, the regular expressions

\mathsf{L}_{1}
and
\mathsf{L}_{2}
are
congruent for
\phi
, denoted

\mathsf{L}_{1}\cong_{\scriptstyle{\phi}}\mathsf{L}_{2}
, if and only if

{\langle\!\!\langle}{\mathsf{L}_{1}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})={\langle\!\!\langle}{\mathsf{L}_{2}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})
, for all
configurations
\gamma\in{{\bf[\![}\phi{\bf]\!]}}
.

Despite the universal condition that ranges over a possibly infinite
set of configurations, congruence of regular expressions with alphabet
symbols of the form
\Sigma[{\alpha}]
, where
\alpha
is an
interaction or a predicate atom, is effectively decidable by an
argument similar to the one used to prove equivalence of symbolic
automata
[
25
]
.

5.2
Inference Rules for Havoc Triples

We use judgements of the form
{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi}\}\!\!\}
,
called
havoc triples
, where
\phi
and
\psi
are

CL
formulæ,
\mathsf{L}
is a regular expression, and
\eta
is an

environment
(a set of alphabet symbols), whose role will be
made clear below (Def.
14
and Lemma

3
). A havoc triple states that each finite
sequence of (possibly loose) interactions described by a word in

\mathsf{L}
, when executed in a model of the precondition
\phi
, yields a
model of the postcondition
\psi
.

Definition 10

A havoc triple
{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi}\}\!\!\}
is

valid
, written
\models{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi}\}\!\!\}
if and only if, for each
configuration
\gamma\in{{\bf[\![}\phi{\bf]\!]}}
, each sequence of
interactions
w\in{\langle\!\!\langle}{\mathsf{L}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})
and each configuration

\gamma^{\prime}
, such that
\gamma\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle w}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle w}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle w}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle w}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}_{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\!$\scriptstyle{}$}}\gamma^{\prime}
, we have

\gamma^{\prime}\in{{\bf[\![}\psi{\bf]\!]}}
.

For a symbolic configuration
\phi
, we denote by
\mathrm{inter}({\phi})
and

\mathrm{preds}({\phi})
the sets of interaction and predicate atoms from

\phi
, respectively and define the set of atoms
\mathrm{atoms}({\phi})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\mathrm{inter}({\phi})\cup\mathrm{preds}({\phi})
and the regular expression

\Sigma[{\phi}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\bigcup_{\alpha\in\mathrm{atoms}({\phi})}\Sigma[{\alpha}]
. We show that the validity of a havoc triple is a
sufficient argument for the validity of a havoc query; because havoc
triples are evaluated via open state changes
(Def.
10
), the dual implication is not true, in
general.

Proposition 3

If
\models{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi}\}\!\!\}~{\Sigma[{\phi}]^{*}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi}\}\!\!\}
then

\mathfrak{h}({{\bf[\![}\phi{\bf]\!]}})\subseteq{{\bf[\![}\psi{\bf]\!]}}
.

We describe next a set of axioms and inference rules used to prove the
validity of havoc triples. For a symbolic configuration
\phi
, we
write
x\simeq_{\phi}y
(
x\not\simeq_{\phi}y
) if and only if
the equality (disequality) between
x
and
y
is asserted by the
symbolic configuration
\phi
, e.g.
x\simeq_{\mathsf{emp}*x=z*z=y}y
and
x\not\simeq_{{x}@{\_}*{y}@{\_}}y
; note that
x\not\simeq_{\phi}y
is not necessarily the negation of
x\simeq_{\phi}y
.

Definition 11

For a symbolic configuration
\phi
and an interaction atom

\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle
, we write:

•

{\phi}\dagger{\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle}
if and only if

\phi
contains a subformula
{y}@{q}
, such that
y\simeq_{\phi}x_{i}
and
q
is not the pre-state of some
behavior transition with label
p_{i}
, for some
i=1,2
;
intuitively, any interaction defined by the formula

\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle
is disabled in any model of
\phi
,

•

{\phi}\ddagger{\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle}
if and only
if, for each interaction atom
\langle{{y_{1}}.{\mathit{p^{\prime}_{1}}},{y_{2}}.{\mathit{p^{\prime}_{2}}}}\rangle\in\mathrm{inter}({\phi})
, there exists
i\in[{1},{2}]
, such that
x_{i}\not\simeq_{\phi}y_{i}
; intuitively, the interaction defined by
the formula
\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle
is not already present
in a model of
\phi
i.e.,
\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle*\phi

is satisfiable.

The axioms (Fig.
5
a) discharge valid havoc triples
for the empty sequence (
\epsilon
), that changes nothing and the
sequence consisting of a single interaction atom, that can be either
disabled in every model (
\dagger
), or enabled in some model (
\Sigma
) of
the precondition, respectively; in particular, the (
\Sigma
) axiom
describes the open state change produced by an interaction
(Def.
7
), firing on a (possibly empty) set of components,
whose states match the pre-states of transitions for the associated
behaviors. The (
\bot
) axiom discharges trivially valid triples with
unsatisfiable (false) preconditions.

The redundancy rule (
\mathsf{I}-
) in Fig.
5
b removes an
interaction atom from the precondition of a havoc triple, provided
that the atom is never interpreted as an interaction from the language
denoted by the regular expression from the triple. Conversely, the
rule (
\mathsf{I}+
) adds an interaction to the precondition, provided that the
precondition (with that interaction atom) is consistent. Note that,
without the
{\phi}\ddagger{\alpha}
side condition, we would obtain
a trivial proof for any triple, by adding an interaction atom twice to
the precondition, i.e. using the rule (
\mathsf{I}+
), followed by (
\bot
).

Figure 5
:
Proof System for Havoc Triples

(
\epsilon
)

{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi}\}\!\!\}~{\epsilon}~\{\!\!\{{\phi}\}\!\!\}

(

)

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\alpha=\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle}\\[-1.42262pt]
{{\phi}\dagger{\alpha}}\end{array}

(

)

)

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\alpha=\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle}\\[-1.42262pt]

\end{array}

)

\phi\succeq\varphi

)

)

\mathsf{L}_{1}\cong_{\scriptstyle{\phi}}\mathsf{L}_{2}

)

\mathsf{LU}
)

{i\neq j\in[{1},{k}]}\end{array}
