)

\begin{array}[]{l}{\phi_{i}\simeq\phi_{j}}\\[-1.42262pt]
{i\neq j\in[{1},{k}]}\end{array}

{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\bigwedge_{i=1}^{k}\phi_{i}}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}}~\{\!\!\{{\bigwedge_{i=1}^{k}\psi_{i}}\}\!\!\}

e. Structural Rules

The composition rule (
\bowtie
) splits a proof obligation into two
simpler havoc triples (Fig.
5
c). The pre- and
postconditions of the premisses are subformulæ of the pre- and
postcondition of the conclusion, joined by separating conjunction and
extended by so-called
frontier
formulæ, describing those
sets of interaction atoms that may cross the boundary between the two
separated conjuncts. The frontier formulæ play the role of
environment assumptions in a rely/assume-guarantee style of reasoning

[
58
,
42
]
. They are required for
soundness, under the semantics of open state changes
(Def.
7
), which considers that the interactions can fire
anytime, unless they are explicitly disabled by some component from

\phi_{i}
, for
i=1,2
.

Nevertheless, defining the frontier syntactically faces the following
problem: interactions introduced by a predicate atom in
\phi_{i}
, can
impact the state of a component defined by
\phi_{3-i}
. We tackle
this problem by forbidding predicate atoms that describe
configurations with loose ports, that belong to components lying
outside of the current configuration. We recall that a configuration

(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)
is tight if and only if, for each interaction
(c_{1},p_{1},c_{2},p_{2})\in\mathcal{I}
, we have
c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathcal{C}
. Moreover, we
say that a formula
\varphi
is
tight
if and only if every
model of
\varphi
is tight. For instance, a predicate atom

\mathsf{chain}_{{h},{t}}(x,y)
, for given
h,t\geq 0
(Example
4
)
is tight, because, in each model, the interactions involve only the

\mathit{out}
and
\mathit{in}
ports of adjacent components from the
configuration.

Definition 12
(Frontier)

Given symbolic configurations
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
, the

frontier of
\phi_{i}
and
\phi_{3-i}
is the formula

\mathcal{F}({\phi_{i}},{\phi_{3-i}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\mathop{\scalebox{1.9}{\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$\ast$}}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\!\!\alpha\in\mathrm{inter}({\phi_{3-i}})\setminus(\mathrm{inter}({\overline{\phi}_{3-i}})\cup\mathrm{inter}({\phi_{i}}))}~\alpha
, where
\overline{\phi}_{i}
is the
largest tight subformula of
\phi_{i}
, for
i=1,2
.

Example 8

Let
\phi_{1}=\mathsf{chain}_{{h},{t}}(x,y)*\langle{{y}.{\mathit{out}},{z}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle
and
\phi_{2}=\mathsf{chain}_{{h},{t}}(y,z)*\langle{{x}.{\mathit{out}},{y}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle
. We have

\mathcal{F}({\phi_{1}},{\phi_{2}})=\langle{{x}.{\mathit{out}},{y}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle
and

\mathcal{F}({\phi_{2}},{\phi_{1}})=\langle{{y}.{\mathit{out}},{z}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle
, because the
tightness of
\mathsf{chain}_{{h},{t}}(x,y)
and
\mathsf{chain}_{{h},{t}}(y,z)
means that
the only interactions crossing the boundary of
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}

are the ones described by
\langle{{y}.{\mathit{out}},{z}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle
and

\langle{{x}.{\mathit{out}},{y}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle
.
\blacksquare

Finally, the regular expression of the conclusion of
the (
\bowtie
) rule is the interleaving of the regular expressions from
the premisses, taken with respect to the sets of alphabet symbols

\eta_{i}=\Sigma[{\phi_{i}*\mathcal{F}({\phi_{i}},{\phi_{3-i}})}]
, for

i=1,2
.

The rules in Fig.
5
d introduce regular expressions
built using concatenation, Kleene star and union. In particular, for
reasons related to the soundness of the proof system, the
concatenation rule (
\cdot
) applies to havoc triples whose
preconditions are finite disjunctions of symbolic configurations,
sharing the same structure of component, interaction and predicate
atoms, whereas the cut formulæ (postcondition of the left and
precondition of the right premisse) share the same structure as the
precondition. We formalize below the fact that two formulæ share
the same structure:

Definition 13

Two formulæ
\phi
and
\psi

share the same structure
,
denoted
\phi\simeq\psi
if and only if they become equivalent
when every component atom
{x}@{q}
is replaced by the formula

{x}@{\_}
, in both
\phi
and
\psi
. We write
\phi\succeq\psi
if and only if
\phi
is satisfiable and
\psi
is not, or
else
\phi\simeq\psi
.

The (
\subset
) rule is the dual of (
\cup
), that restricts the language from
the conclusion to a subset of the one from the premisse. As a remark,
by applying the (
\mathsf{I}+
) and (
\subset
) rules in any order, one can derive the
havoc invariance of the intermediate assertions in a single-reversal
reconfiguration sequence (see Def.
6
and
Prop.
1
). The rule (
\cong
) substitutes a
regular expression with a congruent one, with respect to the
precondition.

Last, the rules in Fig.
5
e modify the structure of
the pre- and postconditions. In particular, the left unfolding rule
(
\mathsf{LU}
) has a premisse for each step of unfolding of a predicate atom
from the conclusion’s precondition, with respect to a rule from the
SID. The environment and the regular expression in each premisse are
obtained by replacing the alphabet symbol of the unfolded predicate
symbol by the set of alphabet symbols from the unfolding step, where

\mathsf{L}[\Sigma[{\alpha}]/\mathsf{L}^{\prime}]
denotes the regular expression obtained
by replacing each occurrence of the alphabet symbol
\Sigma[{\alpha}]

in
\mathsf{L}
with the regular expression
\mathsf{L}^{\prime}
.

5.3
Havoc Proofs

A
proof tree
is a finite tree
T
whose nodes are labeled by
havoc triples and, for each node
n
not on the frontier of
T
, the
children of
n
are the premisses of the application of a rule from
Fig.
5
, whose conclusion is the label of
n
. For
the purposes of this paper, we consider only proof trees that meet the
following condition:

Assumption 1

The root of the proof tree is labeled by a havoc triple

{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi}\}\!\!\}
, such that
\phi
is a
symbolic configuration and
\eta=\{{\Sigma[{\alpha}]\mid\alpha\in\mathrm{atoms}({\phi})}\}
.

It is easy to check that the above condition on the shape of the
precondition and the relation between the precondition and the
environment holds recursively, for the labels of all nodes in a proof
tree that meets assumption
1
. Before tackling the
soundness of the havoc proof system (Fig.
5
), we
state an invariance property of the environments of havoc triples that
occur in a proof tree:

Definition 14

A havoc triple
{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi}\}\!\!\}
is

distinctive
if and only if

{\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\alpha_{1}}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})\cap{\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\alpha_{2}}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})=\emptyset
, for all

\Sigma[{\alpha_{1}}],\Sigma[{\alpha_{2}}]\in\eta
and all
\gamma\in{{\bf[\![}\phi{\bf]\!]}}
.

The next lemma is proved inductively on the structure of the proof
tree, using Assumption
1
.

Lemma 3

Given a proof tree
T
, each node in
T
is labeled with a
distinctive havoc triple.

In order to deal with inductively defined predicates that occur within
the pre- and postconditions of the havoc triples, we use cyclic proofs

[
14
]
. A
cyclic proof tree

T
is a proof tree
such that every node on the frontier is either the conclusion of an
axiom in Fig.
5
a, or there is another node
m

whose label matches the label of
n
via a substitution of variables;
we say that
n
is a
bud
and
m
is its
companion
. A
cyclic proof tree is a
cyclic proof
if and only if every
infinite path through the proof tree extended with bud-companion
edges, goes through the conclusion of a (
\mathsf{LU}
) rule infinitely
often
6
6

6

This condition can be effectively decided by checking
the emptiness of a Büchi automaton
[
14
]
.
. We
denote by
\Vdash{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi}\}\!\!\}
the fact that

{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi}\}\!\!\}
labels the root of a cyclic
proof and state the following soundness theorem:

Theorem 2

If
\Vdash{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi}\}\!\!\}
then

\models{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi}\}\!\!\}
.

The proof is by induction on the structure of the proof tree, using
Lemma
3
.

5.4
A Havoc Proof Example

We demonstrate the use of the proof system in
Fig.
5
on the havoc invariance side conditions
required by the reconfiguration proofs from
§
4.3
. In fact, we prove a more general
statement, namely that
\mathsf{chain}_{{h},{t}}(x,y)
is havoc invariant, for all

h,t\geq 0
. An immediate consequence is that
\mathsf{chain}_{{1},{1}}(z,x)
is
havoc invariant. In particular, the havoc invariance proof for

{y}@{\mathsf{H}}*\langle{{y}.{\mathit{out}},{z}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*\mathsf{chain}_{{1},{1}}(z,x)

is an instance of the subgoal (
A
) below, whereas the proof
for
{y}@{\mathsf{H}}*\mathsf{chain}_{{1},{1}}(z,x)
can be obtained by
applying rules (
\mathsf{I}+
) and (
\subset
) to (
A
), for
h=t=1
.

For space reasons, we introduce backlinks from buds to companions
whose labels differ by a renaming of free variables and of the
h
and

t
indices in
\mathsf{chain}_{{h},{t}}
, such that each pair
(h^{\prime},t^{\prime})
in the
label of a companion is lexicographically smaller or equal to a pair

(h,t)
in the bud. This is a compact (folded) representation of a
cyclic proof tree, obtained by repeatedly appending the subtree rooted
at the companion to the bud, until all buds are labeled with triples
that differ from their companion’s only by a renaming of free
variables
7
7

7

This is bound to happen, because a pair
(h,t)
of
positive integers cannot be decreased indefinitely.
. Note that such
folding is only possible because the definitions of

\mathsf{chain}_{{h},{t}}(x,y)
and
\mathsf{chain}_{{h^{\prime}},{t^{\prime}}}(x,y)
, for
h,t,h^{\prime},t^{\prime}\geq 1

are the same, up to the indices of the predicate symbols (Example

4
).

(
\epsilon
)

{\emptyset}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{{x}@{\_}}\}\!\!\}~{\epsilon}~\{\!\!\{{{x}@{\_}}\}\!\!\}

(
\epsilon
)

{\emptyset}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{{x}@{\mathsf{H}}}\}\!\!\}~{\epsilon}~\{\!\!\{{{x}@{\mathsf{H}}}\}\!\!\}

(
\epsilon
)

{\emptyset}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{{x}@{\mathsf{T}}}\}\!\!\}~{\epsilon}~\{\!\!\{{{x}@{\mathsf{T}}}\}\!\!\}

(A)    (B)

(
\mathsf{LU}
)

{\{{\Sigma[{\mathsf{chain}_{{h},{t}}(z,x)}]}\}}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{chain}_{{h},{t}}(z,x)}\}\!\!\}~{\Sigma[{\mathsf{chain}_{{h},{t}}(z,x)}]^{*}}~\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{chain}_{{h},{t}}(z,x)}\}\!\!\}

(1)

In the proof of the subgoal
(A)
below, alphabet
symbols are abbreviated as
{\Sigma_{z,y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\Sigma[{\langle{{z}.{\mathit{out}},{y}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle}]
and
{\Sigma_{y,x}^{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\Sigma[{\mathsf{chain}_{{h\dot{-}1},{t}}(y,x)}]
. We use the following congruence (Def.
9
):

({\Sigma_{z,y}}\cup{\Sigma_{y,x}^{1}})^{*}\cong_{\scriptstyle{{z}@{\mathsf{H}}*\langle{{z}.{\mathit{out}},{y}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*\mathsf{chain}_{{h\dot{-}1},{t}}(y,x)}}{{\Sigma_{y,x}^{1}}^{*}\cup[{\Sigma_{y,x}^{1}}^{*}\cdot{\Sigma_{z,y}}\cdot({\Sigma_{z,y}}\cup{\Sigma_{y,x}^{1}})^{*}]}

The rule (
\mathsf{C}
) strenghtens the
postcondition
\mathsf{chain}_{{h},{t}}(z,x)
to an unfolding
\mathsf{chain}_{{h},{t}}(z,x)\Leftarrow\exists y~.~{z}@{\mathsf{H}}*\langle{{z}.{\mathit{out}},{y}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle*\mathsf{chain}_{{h\dot{-}1},{t}}(y,x)
, whose
existentially quantified variable is, moreover, bound to the free
variable
y
from the precondition. The frontier formulæ in the
application of rule (
\bowtie
) are

\mathcal{F}({{z}@{\mathsf{H}}},{\mathsf{chain}_{{h\dot{-}1},{t}}(y,x)})=\mathcal{F}({\mathsf{chain}_{{h\dot{-}1},{t}}(y,x)},{{z}@{\mathsf{H}}})=\mathsf{emp}
.

(
\epsilon
)

{\emptyset}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{{z}@{\mathsf{H}}}\}\!\!\}~{\epsilon}~\{\!\!\{{{z}@{\mathsf{H}}}\}\!\!\}

backlink to
(1)

{\{{{\Sigma_{y,x}^{1}}}\}}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{chain}_{{h\dot{-}1},{t}}(y,x)}\}\!\!\}~{{\Sigma_{y,x}^{1}}^{*}}~\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{chain}_{{h\dot{-}1},{t}}(y,x)}\}\!\!\}

(
\bowtie
)

{\{{{\Sigma_{y,x}^{1}}}\}}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{{z}@{\mathsf{H}}\ast\mathsf{chain}_{{h\dot{-}1},{t}}(y,x)}\}\!\!\}~{{\Sigma_{y,x}^{1}}^{*}}~\{\!\!\{{{z}@{\mathsf{H}}\ast\mathsf{chain}_{{h\dot{-}1},{t}}(y,x)}\}\!\!\}

(
\mathsf{I}-
)

(A1)

{\{{{\Sigma_{z,y}},{\Sigma_{y,x}^{1}}}\}}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{{z}@{\mathsf{H}}\ast\langle{{z}.{\mathit{out}},{y}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle\ast\mathsf{chain}_{{h\dot{-}1},{t}}(y,x)}\}\!\!\}~{{\Sigma_{y,x}^{1}}^{*}}~\{\!\!\{{{z}@{\mathsf{H}}\ast\langle{{z}.{\mathit{out}},{y}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle\ast\mathsf{chain}_{{h\dot{-}1},{t}}(y,x)}\}\!\!\}

(
\mathsf{C}
)

{\{{{\Sigma_{z,y}},{\Sigma_{y,x}^{1}}}\}}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{{z}@{\mathsf{H}}\ast\langle{{z}.{\mathit{out}},{y}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle\ast\mathsf{chain}_{{h\dot{-}1},{t}}(y,x)}\}\!\!\}~{{\Sigma_{y,x}^{1}}^{*}}~\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{chain}_{{h},{t}}(z,x)}\}\!\!\}

(A2)

(
\cup
)

{\{{{\Sigma_{z,y}},{\Sigma_{y,x}^{1}}}\}}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{{z}@{\mathsf{H}}\ast\langle{{z}.{\mathit{out}},{y}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle\ast\mathsf{chain}_{{h\dot{-}1},{t}}(y,x)}\}\!\!\}~{{\Sigma_{y,x}^{1}}^{*}\cup[{\Sigma_{y,x}^{1}}^{*}\cdot{\Sigma_{z,y}}\cdot({\Sigma_{z,y}}\cup{\Sigma_{y,x}^{1}})^{*}]}~\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{chain}_{{h},{t}}(z,x)}\}\!\!\}

)

{\textbf{(A)}~\{{{\Sigma_{z,y}},{\Sigma_{y,x}^{1}}}\}}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{{z}@{\mathsf{H}}\ast\langle{{z}.{\mathit{out}},{y}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle\ast\mathsf{chain}_{{h\dot{-}1},{t}}(y,x)}\}\!\!\}~{({\Sigma_{z,y}}\cup{\Sigma_{y,x}^{1}})^{*}}~\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{chain}_{{h},{t}}(z,x)}\}\!\!\}

backlink to
(A1)

{\{{{\Sigma_{z,y}},{\Sigma_{y,x}^{1}}}\}}\triangleright~\{\!\!\{{{z}@{\mathsf{H}}\ast\langle{{z}.{\mathit{out}},{y}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle\ast\mathsf{chain}_{{h\dot{-}1},{t}}(y,x)}\}\!\!\}

{{\Sigma_{y,x}^{1}}^{*}}~\{\!\!\{{{z}@{\mathsf{H}}\ast\langle{{z}.{\mathit{out}},{y}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle\ast\mathsf{chain}_{{h\dot{-}1},{t}}(y,x)}\}\!\!\}

(A3)

(A4)

(
\cdot
)

{\{{{\Sigma_{z,y}},{\Sigma_{y,x}^{1}}}\}}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{{z}@{\mathsf{H}}\ast\langle{{z}.{\mathit{out}},{y}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle\ast\mathsf{chain}_{{h\dot{-}1},{t}}(y,x)}\}\!\!\}~{{\Sigma_{y,x}^{1}}^{*}\cdot{\Sigma_{z,y}}\cdot({\Sigma_{z,y}}\cup{\Sigma_{y,x}^{1}})^{*}}~\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{false}}\}\!\!\}

(
\mathsf{C}
)

{\textbf{(A2)}~\{{{\Sigma_{z,y}},{\Sigma_{y,x}^{1}}}\}}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{{z}@{\mathsf{H}}\ast\langle{{z}.{\mathit{out}},{y}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle\ast\mathsf{chain}_{{h\dot{-}1},{t}}(y,x)}\}\!\!\}~{{\Sigma_{y,x}^{1}}^{*}\cdot{\Sigma_{z,y}}\cdot({\Sigma_{z,y}}\cup{\Sigma_{y,x}^{1}})^{*}}~\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{chain}_{{h},{t}}(z,x)}\}\!\!\}

(

)

{\textbf{(A3)}~\{{{\Sigma_{z,y}},{\Sigma_{y,x}^{1}}}\}}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{{z}@{\mathsf{H}}\ast\langle{{z}.{\mathit{out}},{y}.{\mathit{in}}}\rangle\ast\mathsf{chain}_{{h\dot{-}1},{t}}(y,x)}\}\!\!\}~{{\Sigma_{z,y}}}~\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{false}}\}\!\!\}

(
\bot
)

{\textbf{(A4)}~\{{{\Sigma_{z,y}},{\Sigma_{y,x}^{1}}}\}}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{false}}\}\!\!\}~{({\Sigma_{z,y}}\cup{\Sigma_{y,x}^{1}})^{*}}~\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{false}}\}\!\!\}

For space reasons, the proof of the subgoal
(B)
is
provided as supplementary material.

6
A Worked-out Example: Reconfigurable Tree Architectures

In addition to token rings (Fig.
1
), we apply our
method to reconfiguration scenarios of distributed systems with
tree-shaped architectures. Such (virtual) architectures are e.g. used
in flooding and leader election algorithms. They are applicable, for
instance, when every component in the system must notify a designated
controller, placed in the root of the tree, about an event that
involves each component from the frontier of the tree. Conversely, the
root component may need to notify the rest of the components. The tree
architecture guarantees that the notification phase takes time

\mathcal{O}(\log n)
in the number
n
of components in the tree, when the
tree is balanced, i.e. the lengths of the longest and shortest paths
between the root and the frontier differ by at most a constant
factor. A reconfiguration of a tree places a designated component
(whose priority has increased dynamically) closer to the frontier
(dually, closer to the root) in order to receive the notification
faster. In balanced trees, reconfigurations involve
structure-preserving rotations. For instance,
self-adjustable
splay-tree networks

[
62
]
use the
zig
(left
rotation),
zig-zig
(left-left rotation) and
zig-zag

(left-right rotation) operations
[
65
]
to move nodes
in the tree, while keeping the balance between the shortest and
longest paths.

Fig.
6
shows a model of reconfigurable tree
architectures, in which each leaf component starts in state

\mathit{leaf\!\_busy}
and sends a notification to its parent before
entering the
\mathit{leaf\!\_idle}
state. An inner component starts in
state
\mathit{idle}
and waits for notifications from both its left
(
\mathit{r_{\ell}}
) and right (
\mathit{r_{r}}
) children before sending a notification
to its parent (
\mathit{s}
), unless this component is the root
(Fig.
6
a). We model notifications by interactions of the form

\langle{{\_}.{\mathit{\mathit{s}}},{\_}.{\mathit{\mathit{r_{\ell}}}}}\rangle
and
\langle{{\_}.{\mathit{\mathit{s}}},{\_}.{\mathit{\mathit{r_{r}}}}}\rangle
.
The notification phase is completed when the
root is in state
\mathit{right}
, every inner component is in the

\mathit{idle}
state and every leaf is in the
\mathit{leaf\!\_idle}
state.

Fig.
6
b shows a
right rotation
that reverses the
positions of components with identifiers
x
and
y
, implemented by
the reconfiguration program from Fig.
7
. The
rotation applies only to configurations in which both
x
and
y
are
in state
\mathit{idle}
, by distinguishing the case when
y
is a left
or a right child of
z
. For simplicity, Fig.
7

shows the program in case
y
is a left child, the other case being
symmetric. Note that, applying the rotation in a configuration where
the component indexed by
x
is in state
\mathit{right}

(both
a
and
b
have sent their notifications to
x
) and the one
indexed by
y
is in state
\mathit{idle}
(
c
has not yet sent its
notification to
y
) yields a configuration from which
c
cannot send
its notification further, because
x
has now become the root of the
subtree changed by the rotation (a similar scenario is when
y
is in
state
\mathit{right}
,
x
is in state
\mathit{idle}
and
a
,
b
and
c

have sent their notifications to their parents).

We prove that,
whenever a right rotation is applied to a tree,
such that the subtrees rooted at
a
,
b
and
c
have not sent
their notifications yet, the result is another tree in which the
subtrees rooted at
a
,
b
and
c
are still waiting to submit
their notifications
. This guarantees that the notification phase
will terminate properly with every inner component (except for the
root) in state
\mathit{idle}
and every leaf component in state

\mathit{leaf\!\_idle}
, even if one or more reconfigurations take place in
between. In particular, this proves the correctness of more complex
reconfigurations of splay tree architectures, using e.g. the zig-zig
and zig-zag operations
[
62
]
.

The proof in Fig.
7
uses the inductive definitions
from Fig.
6
c. The predicates
\mathsf{tree}_{\mathit{idle}}(x)
,

\mathsf{tree}_{\neg\mathit{idle}}(x)
define trees where all components are idle, and
where some notifications are still being propagated, respectively. The
predicate
\mathsf{tree}(x)
conveys no information about the states of the
components and the predicate
\mathsf{tseg}(x,u)
defines a tree segment,
from component
x
to component
u
. To use the havoc proof system
from Fig.
5
, we need the following
statement
8
8

8

This is similar to
Prop.
2
.
:

Proposition 4

The set of symbolic configurations using predicate atoms

\mathsf{tree}_{\mathit{idle}}(x)
,
\mathsf{tree}_{\neg\mathit{idle}}(x)
,
\mathsf{tree}(x)
and
\mathsf{tseg}(x,y)
is
precisely closed.

Moreover, each predicate atom
\mathsf{tree}_{\mathit{idle}}(x)
,
\mathsf{tree}_{\neg\mathit{idle}}(x)
,

\mathsf{tree}(x)
and
\mathsf{tseg}(x,y)
is tight, because, in each model of
these atoms, the interactions
\langle{{u}.{\mathit{\mathit{s}}},{v}.{\mathit{\mathit{r_{\ell}}}}}\rangle
and
\langle{{u}.{\mathit{\mathit{s}}},{v}.{\mathit{\mathit{r_{r}}}}}\rangle

are between the ports
\langle{\mathit{s},\mathit{r_{\ell}}}\rangle
and

\langle{\mathit{s},\mathit{r_{r}}}\rangle
of the components

u
and
v
, respectively.

The precondition of the reconfiguration program in
Fig.
7
states that
x
and
y
are idle components,
and the
a
,
b
and
c
subtrees are not idle, whereas the
postcondition states that the
x
subtree is not idle. As mentioned,

this is sufficient to guarantee the correct termination of the
notification phase after the right rotation
. As in the proofs from
§
4.3
, proving the correctness of the
sequential composition of primitive commands requires proving the
havoc invariance of the annotations. However, since in this case, the
reconfiguration sequence is single-reversal (Def.

6
), we are left with proving havoc invariance
only for the annotations marked with
(\sharp)
in
Fig.
7
(Prop.
1
). For
space reasons, the havoc invariance proofs of these annotations are
provided as supplementary material.

7
Towards Automated Proof Generation

Proof generation can be automated, by tackling the following technical
problems, briefly described in this section.

The entailment problem

Given a SID
\Delta
and two
CL
formulæ

\phi
and
\psi
, interpreted over
\Delta
, is every model of
\phi

also a model of
\psi
? This problem arises e.g., when applying the
rule of consequence (Fig.
4
c bottom-left) in a
Hoare-style proof of a reconfiguration program. Unsurprisingly, the

CL
entailment inherits the positive and negative aspects of the

SL
entailment
[
61
]
. For instance, one can reduce the
undecidable problem of universality of context-free languages

[
5
]
to
CL
entailment, with
\phi
and
\psi

restricted to predicate atoms. Decidability can be recovered via two
restrictions on the syntax of the rules in the SID and a semantic
restriction on the configurations that occur as models of the
predicate atoms defined by the SID. The syntactic restrictions are
that, each rule is of the form
\mathsf{A}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\#(\mathsf{A})})\leftarrow\exists y_{1}\ldots\exists y_{m}~.~{x}@{q}*\phi*\mathop{\scalebox{1.9}{\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$\ast$}}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\ell=1}^{h}\mathsf{B}^{\ell}(z^{\ell}_{1},\ldots,z^{\ell}_{\#(\mathsf{B}^{\ell})})
,
where
\phi
consists of interaction atoms, such that:
(1)
x_{1}
occurs in each interaction atom from
\phi
,
(2)
\bigcup_{ell=1}^{h}\{{z^{\ell}_{1},\ldots,z^{\ell}_{\#(\mathsf{B}^{\ell})}}\}=\{{x_{2},\ldots,x_{\#(\mathsf{A})}}\}\cup\{{y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}}\}
, and
(3) for each
\ell\in[{1},{h}]
,
z^{\ell}_{1}
occurs in
\phi
.
Furthermore, the semantic restriction is that, in each model of a
predicate atom, a component must occur in a
bounded number of
interactions
, i.e., the structure is a graph of bounded degree. For
instance, star topologies with a central controller and an unbounded
number of workers can be defined in
CL
, but do not satisfy this
constraint. With these restrictions, it can be shown that the

CL
entailment problem is
2\mathsf{EXP}
-complete, thus matching the
complexity of the similar problem for

SL

[
31
,
43
]
. Technical are
given in
[
9
]
.

The frame inference problem

Given two

CL
formulæ
\phi
and
\psi
find a formula
\varphi
, such
that
\phi\models\psi*\varphi
. This problem occurs e.g., when
applying the frame rule (Fig.
4
c bottom-right) with a
premisse
\{{\phi}\}~{\mathsf{R}}~\{{\psi}\}
to an arbitrary precondition

\xi
i.e., one must infer a frame
\varphi
such that
\xi\models\phi*\varphi
. This problem has been studied for

SL

[
17
,
40
]
,
in cases where the SID defines only data structures of a restricted
form (typically nested lists). Reconsidering the frame inference
problem for
CL
is of paramount importance for automating the
generation of Hoare-style correctness proofs and is an open problem.

Automating havoc invariance proofs

Given a precondition
\phi
and a regular expression
\mathsf{L}
, the
parallel composition rule (
\bowtie
) requires the inference of regular
expressions
\mathsf{L}_{1}
and
\mathsf{L}_{2}
, such that
\mathsf{L}_{1}\bowtie_{\eta_{1},\eta_{2}}\mathsf{L}_{2}\cong_{\scriptstyle{\phi}}\mathsf{L}
. We conjecture
that, under the bounded degree restriction above, the languages of the
frontier (cross-boundary) interactions (Def.
12
) are
regular and can be automatically inferred by classical automata
construction techniques.

8
Related Work

The ability of reconfiguring coordinating architectures of software
systems has received much interest in the Software Engineering
community, see the surveys

[
12
,
16
]
. We consider

programmed reconfiguration
, in which the architecture changes
occur according to a sequential program, executed in parallel with the
system to which reconfiguration applies. The languages used to write
such programs are classified according to the underlying formalism
used to define their operational semantics:
process algebras
,
e.g.
\pi
-
ADL

[
20
]
,
darwin

[
51
]
,
hyper-graphs
and
graph
rewriting

[
66
,
69
,
50
,
19
,
3
]
,

chemical reactions

[
68
]
, etc. We
separate architectures (structures) from behaviors, thus relating to
the BIP framework
[
6
]
and its extensions for
dynamic reconfigurable systems DR-BIP
[
32
]
. In a similar
vein, the REO language
[
4
]
supports reconfiguration by
changing the structure of connectors
[
24
]
.

Checking the correctness of a dynamically reconfigurable system
considers mainly
runtime verification
methods, i.e. checking a
given finite trace of observed configurations against a logical
specification. For instance, in
[
15
]
, configurations
are described by annotated hyper-graphs and configuration invariants
of finite traces, given first-order logic, are checked using
Alloy

[
41
]
. More recently,

[
30
,
49
,
33
]
apply
temporal logic to runtime verification of reconfigurable systems.
Model checking of temporal specifications is also applied to REO
programs, under simplifying assumption that render the system
finite-state
[
24
]
. In contrast, we use induction to deal
with parameterized systems of unbounded sizes.

To the best of our knowledge, our work is the first to tackle the

verification
of reconfiguration programs, by formally proving
the absence of bugs, using a Hoare-style annotation of a
reconfiguration program with assertions that describe infinite sets of
configurations, with unboundedly many components. Traditionally,
reasoning about the correctness of unbounded networks of parallel
processes uses mostly hard-coded architectures (see

[
7
]
for a survey),
whereas the more recently developed architecture description logics

[
44
,
52
]
do not consider the
reconfigurability aspect of distributed systems.

Specifying parameterized component-based systems by inductive
definitions is not new.
Network grammars

[
64
]
use context-free grammar rules to describe
systems with linear (pipeline, token-ring) structure, obtained by
composition of an unbounded number of processes. More complex
structures are specified recursively using graph grammar rules with
parameter variables
[
50
]
. To avoid clashes, these
variables must be renamed to unique names and assigned unique indices
at each unfolding step. Our recursive specifications use existential
quantifiers to avoid name clashes and separating conjunction to
guarantee that the components and interactions obtained by the
unfolding of the rules are unique.

The assertion language introduced in this paper is a resource logic
that supports local reasoning
[
56
]
. Local
reasoning about parallel programs has been traditionally within the
scope of Concurrent Separation Logic (CSL), that introduced a parallel
composition rule
[
55
]
, with a
non-interfering (race-free) semantics of shared-memory parallelism

[
13
]
. Considering interference in CSL requires more
general proof rules, combining ideas of assume- and rely-guarantee

[
58
,
42
]
with local reasoning

[
35
,
67
]
and abstract notions of framing

[
28
,
27
,
34
]
. These rules
generalize from both standard CSL parallel composition and
rely-guarantee rules, allowing even to reason about properties of
concurrent objects, such as (non-)linearizability

[
63
]
. However, the body of work on CSL deals almost
entirely with shared-memory multithreading programs, instead of
distributed systems, which is the aim of our work. In contrast, we
develop a resource logic in which the processes do not just share and
own resources, but become mutable resources themselves.

9
Conclusions and Future Work

We present a framework for deductive verification of reconfiguration
programs, based on a configuration logic that supports local
reasoning. We prove the absence of design bugs in ideal networks,
without packet loss and communication delays, using a discrete
event-based model of behavior, the usual level of abstraction in
formal verification of parameterized distributed systems. Our
configuration logic relies on inductive predicates to describe systems
with unbounded number of components. It is used to annotate
reconfiguration programs with Hoare triples, whose validity relies on
havoc invariants about the ongoing interactions in the system. These
invariants are tackled with a specific proof system, that uses a
parallel composition rule in the style of assume/rely-guarantee
reasoning.

As future work, we consider push-button techniques for frame inference
and havoc invariant synthesis, allowing broadcast interactions between
all the components, and extensions of the finite-state model of
behavior, using timed and hybrid automata.

. By induction on the structure of the local

. For the base case

{\langle\!\langle}\mathsf{new}(q,x){\rangle\!\rangle}(\gamma_{1}\bullet\gamma_{2})=

\begin{array}[]{l}\{{(\mathcal{C}_{1}\cup\mathcal{C}_{2}\cup\{{c}\},\mathcal{I}_{1}\cup\mathcal{I}_{2},(\varrho_{1}\cup\varrho_{2})[c\leftarrow q],\nu[x\leftarrow c])}\}=\\
\{{(\mathcal{C}_{1}\cup\{{c}\},\mathcal{I}_{1},\varrho_{1}[c\leftarrow q],\nu[x\leftarrow c])\bullet(\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{I}_{2},\varrho_{2},\nu[x\leftarrow c])}\}\subseteq\\
{\langle\!\langle}\mathsf{new}(q,x){\rangle\!\rangle}(\gamma_{1})\bullet{\big\{\gamma_{2}\big\}}\!\!\uparrow^{\scriptscriptstyle{\{{x}\}}}={\langle\!\langle}\mathsf{new}(q,x){\rangle\!\rangle}(\gamma_{1})\bullet{\big\{\gamma_{2}\big\}}\!\!\uparrow^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{modif}({\mathsf{new}(q,x)})}}\end{array}

•

\mathsf{R}=\mathsf{delete}(x)
: we distinguish the following cases:

–

\nu(x)\in\mathcal{C}_{1}
, we compute

\begin{array}[]{l}\{{((\mathcal{C}_{1}\cup\mathcal{C}_{2})\setminus\{{\nu(x)}\},\mathcal{I}_{1}\cup\mathcal{I}_{2},\varrho_{1}\cup\varrho_{2},\nu)}\}=\\
\{{(\mathcal{C}_{1}\setminus\{{\nu(x)}\},\mathcal{I}_{1},\varrho_{1},\nu)}\}\bullet\{{(\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{I}_{2},\varrho_{2},\nu)}\}\subseteq\\

, thus

\begin{array}[]{l}\{{(\mathcal{C}_{1}\cup\mathcal{C}_{2},(\mathcal{I}_{1}\cup\mathcal{I}_{2})\setminus\{{(\nu(x_{1}),p_{1},\nu(x_{2}),p_{2})}\},\varrho_{1}\cup\varrho_{2},\nu)}\}=\\

Proof
. In order to apply the sequential
composition rule for the entire sequence, we need to prove that

\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{\ell-1}
are havoc invariant:

•

For
i\in[{1},{k}]
, the proof is by induction on

i

\phi_{1}
is havoc invariant, by the

and
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)\xRightarrow{{\scriptstyle}}_{\raisebox{4.0pt}{\!$\scriptstyle{}$}}\ldots\xRightarrow{{\scriptstyle}}_{\raisebox{4.0pt}{\!$\scriptstyle{}$}}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho^{\prime},\nu)
be a sequence of state changes induced by the
execution of some interactions
(c_{i_{1}},p_{i_{1}},c^{\prime}_{i_{1}},p^{\prime}_{i_{1}})
,
\ldots

\models\{{\phi_{i-1}}\}~{\mathsf{disconnect}(x_{i}.p_{i},x^{\prime}_{i}.p^{\prime}_{i})}~\{{\phi_{i}}\}

(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\varrho,\nu)
of
\phi_{i-1}
, such that

(c_{i_{1}},p_{i_{1}},c^{\prime}_{i_{1}},p^{\prime}_{i_{1}})
,
\ldots
,
(c_{i_{n}},p_{i_{n}},c^{\prime}_{i_{n}},p^{\prime}_{i_{n}})\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}

\phi_{i-1}

(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\varrho^{\prime},\nu)\models\phi_{i-1}

(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho^{\prime},\nu)\models\phi_{i}
. Since the

(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)
and

were arbitrary, we
obtain that
\phi_{i}
is havoc invariant.

•

For
i\in[{k+1},{\ell-1}]

induction on
i
. In the base case,
\phi_{\ell-1}
is havoc
invariant, by the hypothesis. For the inductive step, let
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)\models\phi_{i-1}
and
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)\xRightarrow{{\scriptstyle}}_{\raisebox{4.0pt}{\!$\scriptstyle{}$}}\ldots\xRightarrow{{\scriptstyle}}_{\raisebox{4.0pt}{\!$\scriptstyle{}$}}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho^{\prime},\nu)
be a sequence of state changes, induced by the executions
of some interactions
(c_{i_{1}},p_{i_{1}},c^{\prime}_{i_{1}},p^{\prime}_{i_{1}})
,

\ldots
,
(c_{i_{n}},p_{i_{n}},c^{\prime}_{i_{n}},p^{\prime}_{i_{n}})\in\mathcal{I}
.
Since
\models\{{\phi_{i-1}}\}~{\mathsf{connect}(x_{i}.p_{i},x^{\prime}_{i}.p_{i})}~\{{\phi_{i}}\}
, by the hypothesis, there exists a model

(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\varrho,\nu)
of
\phi_{i}
, such that

(c_{i_{1}},p_{i_{1}},c^{\prime}_{i_{1}},p^{\prime}_{i_{1}})
,
\ldots
,
(c_{i_{n}},p_{i_{n}},c^{\prime}_{i_{n}},p^{\prime}_{i_{n}})\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
. Since
\phi_{i}
is

(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\varrho^{\prime},\nu)\models\phi_{i}

,

,
(c_{i_{n}},p_{i_{n}},c^{\prime}_{i_{n}},p^{\prime}_{i_{n}})\}
and
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho^{\prime},\nu)\models\phi_{i-1}

(c_{i_{1}},p_{i_{1}},c^{\prime}_{i_{1}},p^{\prime}_{i_{1}}),\ldots,(c_{i_{n}},p_{i_{n}},c^{\prime}_{i_{n}},p^{\prime}_{i_{n}})
were arbitrary,
\phi_{i-1}
is havoc invariant.
\Box

Proof
. Let
\varphi_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\phi_{i}*\mathop{\scalebox{1.9}{\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$\ast$}}}\hskip 1.0pt_{j=1}^{k_{i}}\mathsf{chain}_{{h_{i,j}},{t_{i,j}}}(x_{i,j},y_{i,j})
be
symbolic configurations, where
\phi_{i}
is a predicate-free symbolic
configuration and
h_{i,j},t_{i,j}\geq 0
are integers, for all
j\in[{1},{k_{i}}]
and
i=1,2
. We prove that
\varphi_{1}
is
precise on
{{\bf[\![}\varphi_{2}{\bf]\!]}}
. Let
\gamma=(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)\in{{\bf[\![}\varphi_{2}{\bf]\!]}}
be a configuration and suppose that there exist
configurations
\gamma^{\prime}=(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\varrho^{\prime},\nu)

and
\gamma^{\prime\prime}=(\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{I}^{\prime\prime},\varrho^{\prime\prime},\nu)
, such
that
\gamma^{\prime}\sqsubseteq\gamma
,
\gamma^{\prime\prime}\sqsubseteq\gamma
,
\gamma^{\prime}\models\varphi_{1}
and
\gamma^{\prime\prime}\models\varphi_{1}
. Then there exist configurations
\gamma^{\prime}_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{0},\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{0},\varrho,\nu),\ldots,\gamma^{\prime}_{k_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{k_{1}},\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{k_{1}},\varrho,\nu)
and

\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}_{0},\mathcal{I}^{\prime\prime}_{0},\varrho,\nu),\ldots,\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{k_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}_{k_{1}},\mathcal{I}^{\prime\prime}_{k_{1}},\varrho,\nu)
, such that:

•

\gamma^{\prime}=\scalebox{1.7}{$\bullet$}_{j=0}^{k_{1}}~\gamma^{\prime}_{j}
and

\gamma^{\prime\prime}=\scalebox{1.7}{$\bullet$}_{j=0}^{k_{1}}~\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{j}
,

•

\gamma^{\prime}_{0}\models\phi_{1}
and
\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{0}\models\phi_{1}
, and

•

\gamma^{\prime}_{j}\models\mathsf{chain}_{{h_{1,j}},{t_{1,j}}}(x_{1,j},y_{1,j})
and
\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{j}\models\mathsf{chain}_{{h_{1,j}},{t_{1,j}}}(x_{1,j},y_{1,j})
, for all

j\in[{1},{k_{1}}]
.

Since
\phi_{1}
is a predicate-free symbolic configuration, we have

\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{0}=\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}_{0}
and
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{0}=\mathcal{I}^{\prime\prime}_{0}
, thus

\gamma^{\prime}_{0}=\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{0}
. Moreover, for each
j\in[{1},{k_{1}}]
, we have
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{j}=\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}_{j}
and
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{j}=\mathcal{I}^{\prime\prime}_{j}
, because both configurations consist of the tight
interactions
(c_{1},\mathit{out},c_{2},\mathit{in})
,
\ldots
,

(c_{\ell-1},\mathit{out},c_{\ell},\mathit{in})
, such that

\nu(x_{1,j})=c_{1}
and
\nu(y_{1,j})=c_{\ell}
. Thus, we
obtain
\gamma^{\prime}_{j}=\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{j}
, for all
j\in[{0},{k}]
,
leading to
\gamma^{\prime}=\gamma^{\prime\prime}
.

Proof
. Let
\gamma=(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)

\phi

\models{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi}\}\!\!\}~{\Sigma[{\phi}]^{*}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi}\}\!\!\}

, for some

, because
\Sigma[{\phi}]=\bigcup_{\alpha\in\mathrm{atoms}({\phi})}\Sigma[{\alpha}]
. Since the choice
of
k\in[{1},{n}]
is arbitrary, we obtain that

.

Proof
. The proof goes by induction on the
structure of the proof tree. For the base case, the tree consists of
a single root node and let
{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi}\}\!\!\}

be the label of the root node. By Assumption
1
,
\phi

is a symbolic configuration and
\eta=\{{\Sigma[{\alpha_{1}}],\ldots,\Sigma[{\alpha_{k}}]}\}
, where
\mathrm{atoms}({\phi})=\{{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}}\}
is the set of interaction and predicate atoms
from
\phi
. Let
\gamma
be a model of
\phi
, hence there exist
configurations
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{k}
, such
that
\gamma=\scalebox{1.7}{$\bullet$}_{i=0}^{k}~\gamma_{i}
and
\gamma_{i}\models\alpha_{i}
, for all
i\in[{1},{k}]
. Because the
composition
\gamma_{i}\bullet\gamma_{j}
is defined, we obtain that

{\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\alpha_{i}}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma_{i}})\cap{\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\alpha_{j}}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma_{j}})=\emptyset
, for all
i\neq j\in[{1},{k}]
. Moreover, since each formula

\alpha_{i}\in\mathrm{atoms}({\phi})
is precise on
{{\bf[\![}\phi{\bf]\!]}}
, we have

{\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\alpha_{i}}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma_{i}})={\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\alpha_{i}}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})
, hence

{\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\alpha_{i}}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})\cap{\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\alpha_{j}}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})=\emptyset
, for all
i\neq j\in[{1},{k}]
. For the inductive step, we distinguish the
cases below, based on the type of the inference rule that expands
the root:

•

(
\mathsf{I}-
) Let
{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi*\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi*\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle}\}\!\!\}
be label of the root of the proof
tree and
\gamma\in{{\bf[\![}\phi*\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle{\bf]\!]}}
be a configuration. Then there
exists configurations
\gamma_{0}
and
\gamma_{1}
, such that

\gamma=\gamma_{0}\bullet\gamma_{1}
,
\gamma_{0}\models\phi
and
\gamma_{1}\models\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle
. By
Assumption
1
, we have
\eta=\Sigma[{\phi}]\cup\{{\Sigma[{\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle}]}\}
. By the inductive
hypothesis, the premiss
{\eta\setminus\{{\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle}\}}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi}\}\!\!\}
of the
rule is distinctive, hence the interpretations of the atoms in the
environment
\{{{\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\alpha}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma_{0}})\mid\alpha\in\mathrm{atoms}({\phi})}\}
are pairwise disjoint. Since each
predicate atom
\alpha\in\mathrm{atoms}({\phi})
is precise on

{{\bf[\![}\phi{\bf]\!]}}
, the sets
{\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\alpha}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})
,

\alpha\in\mathrm{atoms}({\phi})
are also pairwise disjoint. Since

\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle
is precise on
\Gamma
, we
obtain that
{\langle\!\!\langle}{\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma_{1}})={\langle\!\!\langle}{\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})
and, since

\gamma=\gamma_{0}\bullet\gamma_{1}
, the set

{\langle\!\!\langle}{\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})
is disjoint
from the sets
{\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\alpha}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})
,
\alpha\in\mathrm{atoms}({\phi})
, thus
{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi*\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi*\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle}\}\!\!\}
is distinctive.

•

(
\mathsf{I}+
) Let
{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi}\}\!\!\}
be the root
label and let
\gamma\in{{\bf[\![}\phi{\bf]\!]}}
be a configuration. By
Assumption
1
, we have
\eta=\Sigma[{\phi}]
and
let
\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle
be an interaction atom, such
that
{\phi}\ddagger{\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle}
. Let

\gamma^{\prime}
be any model of
\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle
. By

{\phi}\ddagger{\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle}
, we have

\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle\not\in\eta
and, moreover, the

\gamma\bullet\gamma^{\prime}
is defined, thus
\gamma\bullet\gamma^{\prime}\in{{\bf[\![}\phi*\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle{\bf]\!]}}
. By the inductive hypothesis,

{\eta\cup\{{\Sigma[{\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle}]}\}}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi*\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi*\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle}\}\!\!\}
is distinctive, hence

{\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\alpha_{1}}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma\bullet\gamma^{\prime}})\cap{\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\alpha_{2}}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma\bullet\gamma^{\prime}})=\emptyset
, for all
\Sigma[{\alpha_{1}}],\Sigma[{\alpha_{2}}]\in\eta
. Since
\gamma^{\prime}\models\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle
,

\eta=\Sigma[{\phi}]
and

{\phi}\ddagger{\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle}
, we obtain

{\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\alpha}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma\bullet\gamma^{\prime}})={\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\alpha}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})
, for all
\Sigma[{\alpha}]\in\eta
, thus
{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi}\}\!\!\}
is
distinctive.

•

(
\bowtie
) Let
{\eta_{1}\cup\eta_{2}}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi_{1}*\phi_{2}}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}_{1}\bowtie_{\eta_{1},\eta_{2}}\mathsf{L}_{2}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi_{1}*\psi_{2}}\}\!\!\}
be the label of the root,
\eta_{i}=\Sigma[{\phi_{i}*\mathcal{F}({\phi_{i}},{\phi_{3-i}})}]
, for
i=1,2
, and let
\gamma
be a
model of the precondition of this havoc triple. Then there exists
two configurations
\gamma_{1}
,
\gamma_{2}
, such that
\gamma=\gamma_{1}\bullet\gamma_{2}
and
\gamma_{i}\models\phi_{i}
,
for
i=1,2
. By Assumption
1
, we have
\eta_{1}\cup\eta_{2}=\Sigma[{\phi_{1}}]\cup\Sigma[{\phi_{2}}]
. Let

\gamma^{\prime}_{i}
be a structure, such that
\gamma^{\prime}_{i}\sqsubseteq\gamma_{3-i}
and
\gamma^{\prime}_{i}\models\mathcal{F}({\phi_{i}},{\phi_{3-i}})
, for
i=1,2
. By the definition of

\mathcal{F}({\phi_{i}},{\phi_{3-i}})
, as separated conjunction of
interaction atoms from
\phi_{3-i}
, these substructures exist,
and moreover, because each interaction atom is precise on

\Gamma
, they are unique. Then we have
\gamma_{i}\bullet\gamma^{\prime}_{i}\models\phi_{i}*\mathcal{F}({\phi_{i}},{\phi_{3-i}})
, for
i=1,2
. By the inductive hypothesis, since each havoc triple

{\eta_{i}}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi_{i}*\mathcal{F}({\phi_{i}},{\phi_{3-i}})}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}_{i}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi_{i}*\mathcal{F}({\phi_{i}},{\phi_{3-i}})}\}\!\!\}
is distinctive, the sets

\{{{\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\alpha}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma_{i}\bullet\gamma^{\prime}_{i}})\mid\alpha\in\mathrm{atoms}({\phi_{i}})}\}
are pairwise disjoint, for
i=1,2
. Since each predicate atom
\alpha\in\mathrm{atoms}({\phi_{1}*\phi_{2}})
is precise on
{{\bf[\![}\phi_{1}*\phi_{2}{\bf]\!]}}
, hence the sets

\{{{\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\alpha}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})\mid\alpha\in\mathrm{atoms}({\phi_{i}})}\}
are pairwise disjoint as well, for
i=1,2
. Since the configurations
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
share
no interactions, the havoc triple

{\eta_{1}\cup\eta_{2}}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi_{1}*\phi_{2}}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}_{1}\bowtie_{\eta_{1},\eta_{2}}\mathsf{L}_{2}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi_{1}*\psi_{2}}\}\!\!\}
is
distinctive.

(
\mathsf{LU}
) Let
{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi*\mathsf{A}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{\#(\mathsf{A})})}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi}\}\!\!\}
be the label of
the root let
\gamma
be a model of the precondition of this
havoc triple. Then there exist configurations
\gamma_{0}=(\mathcal{C}_{0},\mathcal{I}_{0},\varrho_{0},\nu)

\gamma=\gamma_{0}\bullet\gamma_{1}
,
\gamma_{0}\models\phi

\gamma_{1}\models\mathsf{A}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{\#(\mathsf{A})})
. By

, we have
\eta=\Sigma[{\phi}]\cup\{{\Sigma[{\mathsf{A}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{\#(\mathsf{A})})}]}\}

\mathsf{A}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\#(\mathsf{A})})\leftarrow\exists z_{1}\ldots\exists z_{h}~.~\varphi
in the SID, where

(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{I}_{1},\varrho_{1},\nu[z_{1}\leftarrow c_{1},\ldots,z_{h}\leftarrow c_{h}])\models\varphi
, for some components

\{{z_{1},\ldots,z_{h}}\}\cap\mathrm{fv}({\phi})=\emptyset
(if necessary, by an

and
\gamma^{\prime}_{1}\models\mathsf{A}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{\#(\mathsf{A})})
, where
\gamma^{\prime}_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}(\mathcal{C}_{0},\mathcal{I}_{0},\varrho_{0},\nu[z_{1}\leftarrow c_{1},\ldots,z_{h}\leftarrow c_{h}])
and
\gamma^{\prime}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{I}_{1},\varrho_{1},\nu[z_{1}\leftarrow c_{1},\ldots,z_{h}\leftarrow c_{h}])

\gamma^{\prime}=\gamma^{\prime}_{0}\bullet\gamma^{\prime}_{1}

{\eta^{\prime}}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\exists z_{1}\ldots\exists z_{h}~.~\phi*\varphi[x_{1}/y_{1}\ldots x_{\#(\mathsf{A})}/y_{\#(\mathsf{A})}]}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}^{\prime}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi}\}\!\!\}

\gamma^{\prime}\models\exists z_{1}\ldots\exists z_{h}~.~\phi*\varphi[x_{1}/y_{1}\ldots x_{\#(\mathsf{A})}/y_{\#(\mathsf{A})}]

{\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\alpha_{1}}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma^{\prime}})\cap{\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\alpha_{2}}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma^{\prime}})=\emptyset

and

, we

{\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\alpha_{1}}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})\cap{\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\alpha_{2}}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})=\emptyset
, for all

, leading to the fact that

{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi*\mathsf{A}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{\#(\mathsf{A})})}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi}\}\!\!\}
is distinctive.

(
\vee
) Let
{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}\phi\wedge\delta_{i}}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}}~\{\!\!\{{\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}\psi_{i}}\}\!\!\}
be the label of the root
and let
\gamma
be a model of the precondition of this

\gamma\models\phi\wedge\delta_{i}
, for some

i\in[{1},{k}]
. By the inductive hypothesis, the triple

{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi\wedge\delta_{i}}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi_{i}}\}\!\!\}
is
distinctive, hence
{\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\alpha_{1}}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})\cap{\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\alpha_{1}}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})=\emptyset
, for all

\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathrm{atoms}({\phi})
. Since
\mathrm{atoms}({\phi})=\mathrm{atoms}({\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}\phi\wedge\delta_{i}})
, we obtain that

{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}\phi\wedge\delta_{i}}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}}~\{\!\!\{{\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}\psi_{i}}\}\!\!\}
is distinctive.

•

(
\wedge
) and (
\cdot
): these cases are similar to (
\vee
).

•

(
\mathsf{C}
), (
*
), (
\cup
) and (
\subset
): these cases are trivial,
because the precondition and the environment does not change
between the conclusion and the premisses of these rules.
\Box

See
2

Proof
. For each axiom and inference rule in
Fig.
5
, with premisses

{\eta_{i}}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi_{i}}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}_{i}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi_{i}}\}\!\!\}
, for
i=1,\ldots,k
,
k\geq 0
, and conclusion

{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi}\}\!\!\}
, we prove that:

(\star)~\models{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi}\}\!\!\}\text{, if }\models{\eta_{i}}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi_{i}}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}_{i}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi_{i}}\}\!\!\}\text{, for
all $i\in[{1},{k}]$}

Let us show first that (
\star
) is a
sufficient condition. If
\Vdash{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi}\}\!\!\}
then there exists a cyclic
proof whose root is labeled by

{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi}\}\!\!\}
and we apply the principle
of infinite descent to prove that
\models{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi}\}\!\!\}
. Suppose, for a
contradiction, that this is not the case. Assuming that (
\star
)
holds, each invalid node, with label

{\eta_{i}}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi_{i}}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}_{i}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi_{i}}\}\!\!\}
and counterexample

\gamma_{i}
, not on the frontier of the proof tree, has a
successor, whose label is invalid, for all
i\geq 0
. Let

\mathrm{preds}({\phi_{i}})=\{{\mathsf{A}^{i}_{1}(\mathbf{y}^{i}_{1}),\ldots,\mathsf{A}^{i}_{k_{i}}(\mathbf{y}^{i}_{k_{i}})}\}
be the set of predicate atoms
from
\phi_{i}
, for each
i\geq 0
. Consequently, there exists a set
of configurations
\Gamma_{i}=\{{\gamma^{i}_{0},\ldots,\gamma^{i}_{k_{i}}}\}
, such that
\gamma_{i}=\gamma^{i}_{0}\bullet\ldots\bullet\gamma^{i}_{k_{i}}
and
\gamma^{i}_{j}\models\mathsf{A}^{i}_{j}(\mathbf{y}^{i}_{j})
, for all
j\in[{1},{k_{i}}]
and all
i\geq 0
.

Fact 1

For each
i\geq 0
, either
\Gamma_{i+1}\subseteq\Gamma_{i}
or
there exists
j\in[{1},{k_{i}}]
, such that
\Gamma_{i+1}=\big(\Gamma_{i}\setminus\{{\gamma^{i}_{j}}\}\big)\cup\{{\gamma^{\prime}\in{{\bf[\![}\mathsf{A}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\#(\mathsf{A})}){\bf]\!]}}\mid\gamma^{\prime}\sqsubseteq\gamma^{i}_{j},~\mathsf{A}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\#(\mathsf{A})})\in\mathrm{preds}({\varphi^{i}_{j}[\mathbf{x}^{i}_{j}/\mathbf{y}^{i}_{j}]})}\}
, where

\mathsf{A}^{i}_{j}(\mathbf{x}^{i}_{j})\leftarrow\exists\mathbf{z}^{i}_{j}~.~\varphi^{i}_{j}
is a rule of the SID and
\varphi^{i}_{j}
is a symbolic
configuration.

Proof
. By inspection of the inference rules in
Fig.
5
b-e. The only interesting cases are:

•

(
\bowtie
) in this case
\Gamma_{i+1}\subseteq\Gamma_{i}
,
because the models of the preconditions from the premises are
subconfigurations of the model of the precondition in the
conclusion,

•

(
\mathsf{LU}
) in this case
\Gamma_{i+1}
is obtained by replacing
an element
\gamma^{i}_{j}
from
\Gamma_{i}
with a set of
configurations
\gamma^{\prime}
, such that
\gamma^{\prime}\sqsubseteq\gamma^{i}_{j}
and
\gamma^{\prime}
is a model of a predicate atom
from an unfolding of the predicate atom for which
\gamma^{i}_{j}

is a model.
\Box

For a configuration
\gamma^{i}_{j}\in{{\bf[\![}\mathsf{A}^{i}_{j}(\mathbf{x}^{i}_{j}){\bf]\!]}}
, we denote by
\mathfrak{n}(i,j)
the
minimum number of steps needed to evaluate the
\models
relation in
the given SID. Since
\mathsf{A}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{\#(\mathsf{A})})
is
precise on
{{\bf[\![}\phi*\mathsf{A}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{\#(\mathsf{A})}){\bf]\!]}}
, we
have
{\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\mathsf{A}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{\#(\mathsf{A})})}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma^{\prime}})=\bigcup_{\alpha\in\mathrm{atoms}({\varphi})}{\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\alpha}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma^{\prime}})
. Since

{\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\alpha}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma^{\prime}})={\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\alpha}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})
, for each
\alpha\in\mathrm{atoms}({\phi})\cup\{{\mathsf{A}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{\#(\mathsf{A})})}\}
, we
obtain that
{\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\alpha_{1}}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})\cap{\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\alpha_{2}}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})=\emptyset
, for all

\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathrm{atoms}({\phi})\cup\{{\mathsf{A}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{\#(\mathsf{A})})}\}
, leading to the fact that

{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi*\mathsf{A}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{\#(\mathsf{A})})}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi}\}\!\!\}
is
distinctive. Let
\mathfrak{m}_{i}
be the multiset of numbers

\mathfrak{n}(i,j)
, for all
j\in[{1},{k_{i}}]
and
i\geq 0
. By
Fact
1
, the sequence of multisets
\mathfrak{m}_{0},\mathfrak{m}_{1},\ldots
is such that either
\mathfrak{m}_{i}=\mathfrak{m}_{i+1}
or
\mathfrak{m}_{i}\succ\mathfrak{m}_{i+1}
, where
the Dershowitz-Manna multiset ordering
\prec
is defined as

\mathfrak{m}\prec\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
if and only if there exist two
multisets
X
and
Y
, such that
X\neq\emptyset
,
X\subseteq\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
,
\mathfrak{m}=(\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}\setminus X)\cup Y
, and
for all
y\in Y
there exists some
x\in X
, such that
y<x
. By
the fact that the cyclic proof tree is a cyclic proof, the infinite
path goes infinitely often via a node whose label is the conclusion
of the application of (
\mathsf{LU}
). Then the infinite sequence of multisets

\mathfrak{m}_{0},\mathfrak{m}_{1},\ldots
contains a strictly decreasing
subsequence in the multiset order, which contradicts the fact that

\prec
is well-founded.

Let
\mathfrak{o}{[{w}]}(\gamma)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\{{\gamma^{\prime}\mid\gamma\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle w}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle w}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle w}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle w}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}_{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\!$\scriptstyle{}$}}\gamma^{\prime}}\}
, where the relation
\gamma\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle w}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle w}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle w}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@\dabar@}\limits^{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\,{\scriptstyle w}$}}}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}_{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\!$\scriptstyle{}$}}\gamma^{\prime}
is defined in Def.
7
. We are left
with proving (
\star
) for each type of axiom and inference rule in
Fig.
5
:

•

(

) For each configuration
\gamma
, we have

{\langle\!\!\langle}{\epsilon}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})=\{{\epsilon}\}
and

\mathfrak{o}{[{\epsilon}]}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)=\{{(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)}\}
.

•

(
\dagger
) In any model
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)
of
\phi
, we have

\mathfrak{o}{[{(\nu(x_{1}),p_{1},\nu(x_{2}),p_{2})}]}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)=\emptyset
,
because of the side condition

{\phi}\dagger{\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle}

(Def.
11
).

•

(
\bot
) Because the precondition has no models.

•

(
\Sigma
) By an application of Def.
7
.

•

(
\mathsf{I}-
) Let
\gamma=(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)\in{{\bf[\![}\phi*\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle{\bf]\!]}}
be a configuration. By Lemma

3
, we have that
{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi*\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi*\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle}\}\!\!\}
is distinctive. By the side
condition
\Sigma[{\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle}]\in\eta\setminus\mathrm{supp}({\mathsf{L}})
, it follows that

{\langle\!\!\langle}{\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})
is disjoint
from the interpretation
{\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\alpha}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})
of
any alphabet symbol
\Sigma[{\alpha}]\in\mathrm{supp}({\mathsf{L}})
, hence the
interaction
(\nu(x_{1}),p_{1},\nu(x_{2}),p_{2})
does not occur
in
{\langle\!\!\langle}{\mathsf{L}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})
. By the inductive hypothesis, we
have
\models{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi}\}\!\!\}
, which leads
to the required
\models{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi*\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi*\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle}\}\!\!\}
.

•

(
\mathsf{I}+
) Let
\gamma=(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)\in{{\bf[\![}\phi{\bf]\!]}}
be a
configuration and
\omega\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\Sigma[{\alpha_{1}}]\cdot\ldots\cdot\Sigma[{\alpha_{k}}]
be a finite concatenation of alphabet
symbols from
\mathrm{supp}({\mathsf{L}})
. If
\alpha_{i}=\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle
, for some

,
then we have
{\langle\!\!\langle}{\Sigma[{\alpha_{i}}]}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})=\emptyset
,
because of the side condition

{\phi}\ddagger{\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle}
. Then

\mathfrak{o}{[{w}]}(\gamma)=\emptyset\subseteq{{\bf[\![}\psi{\bf]\!]}}
, for each

. Otherwise, if

\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle
does not occur on
\omega
, then

\mathfrak{o}{[{w}]}(\gamma)\subseteq{{\bf[\![}\psi{\bf]\!]}}
, for each

, by the
inductive hypothesis.

•

(
\bowtie
) Let
\gamma=(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)\in{{\bf[\![}\phi_{1}*\phi_{2}{\bf]\!]}}

be a configuration. Then there exist configurations
\gamma_{i}=(\mathcal{C}_{i},\mathcal{I}_{i},\varrho_{i},\nu)\in{{\bf[\![}\phi_{i}{\bf]\!]}}
,
for
i=1,2
, such that
\gamma=\gamma_{1}\bullet\gamma_{2}
. Let
\gamma^{\prime}_{i}
be configurations such that

\gamma^{\prime}_{i}\sqsubseteq\gamma_{3-i}
and
\gamma^{\prime}_{i}\models\mathcal{F}({\phi_{i}},{\phi_{3-i}})
, for
i=1,2
. Because

\mathcal{F}({\phi_{i}},{\phi_{3-i}})
is a separated conjunction of
interaction atoms, each of which is precise on
\Gamma
, it
follows that
\mathcal{F}({\phi_{i}},{\phi_{3-i}})
is precise on

\Gamma
, thus
\gamma^{\prime}_{i}
are unique, for
i=1,2
. Let

\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\gamma_{i}\bullet\gamma^{\prime}_{3-i}
, for
i=1,2
. Moreover, since
\eta_{i}=\Sigma[{\phi_{i}*\mathcal{F}({\phi_{i}},{\phi_{3-i}})}]
, the only interactions in

{\langle\!\!\langle}{\eta_{i}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})
are the ones in

{\langle\!\!\langle}{\eta_{i}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{i}})
, hence

{\langle\!\!\langle}{\eta_{i}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})={\langle\!\!\langle}{\eta_{i}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{i}})
,
for
i=1,2
. Let
w\in{\langle\!\!\langle}{\mathsf{L}_{1}\bowtie_{\eta_{1},\eta_{2}}\mathsf{L}_{2}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})
be a word. Then

{w}\!\downarrow_{\scriptscriptstyle{{\langle\!\!\langle}{\eta_{i}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})}}\in{\langle\!\!\langle}{\mathsf{L}_{i}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})
, for
i=1,2
. Because

{\langle\!\!\langle}{\eta_{i}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})={\langle\!\!\langle}{\eta_{i}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{i}})
,
we obtain
{w}\!\downarrow_{\scriptscriptstyle{{\langle\!\!\langle}{\eta_{i}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma})}}={w}\!\downarrow_{\scriptscriptstyle{{\langle\!\!\langle}{\eta_{i}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{i}})}}\in{\langle\!\!\langle}{\mathsf{L}_{i}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{i}})
, for
i=1,2
. Since, moreover,

\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{i}\models\phi_{i}*\mathcal{F}({\phi_{i}},{\phi_{3-i}})
, by the
inductive hypothesis we obtain that

\mathfrak{o}{[{{w}\!\downarrow_{\scriptscriptstyle{{\langle\!\!\langle}{\eta_{i}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{i}})}}}]}(\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{i})\subseteq{{\bf[\![}\psi_{i}*\mathcal{F}({\phi_{i}},{\phi_{3-i}}){\bf]\!]}}
, for
i=1,2
. We partition
w=w_{1}w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime\prime}_{1}w^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1}\ldots w_{k}w^{\prime}_{k}w^{\prime\prime}_{k}w^{\prime\prime\prime}_{k}
, for some
k\geq 1
, in three types of (possibly
empty) blocks, such that, for all
j\in[{1},{k}]
, we
have:

–

w_{j}\in\big({\langle\!\!\langle}{\eta_{1}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{1}})\setminus{\langle\!\!\langle}{\eta_{2}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{2}})\big)^{*}
,

–

w^{\prime}_{j},w^{\prime\prime\prime}_{j}\in\big({\langle\!\!\langle}{\eta_{1}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{1}})\cap{\langle\!\!\langle}{\eta_{2}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{2}})\big)^{*}
, and

–

w^{\prime\prime}_{j}\in\big({\langle\!\!\langle}{\eta_{2}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{2}})\setminus{\langle\!\!\langle}{\eta_{1}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{1}})\big)^{*}
.

If
\mathfrak{o}{[{w}]}(\gamma)=\emptyset
, there is nothing to
prove. Otherwise, let
\gamma^{\prime}=(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho^{\prime},\nu)\in\mathfrak{o}{[{w}]}(\gamma)
and

\varrho_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\varrho,\varrho^{\prime}_{1},\varrho^{\prime\prime}_{1},\varrho^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1},\ldots,\varrho_{k},\varrho^{\prime}_{k},\varrho^{\prime\prime}_{k},\varrho^{\prime\prime\prime}_{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\varrho^{\prime}
be a sequence of state maps such
that, for all
j\in[{1},{k}]
, we have

(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho^{\prime}_{j},\nu)\in\mathfrak{o}{[{w_{j}}]}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho_{j},\nu)
,

(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho^{\prime\prime}_{j},\nu)\in\mathfrak{o}{[{w^{\prime}_{j}}]}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho^{\prime}_{j},\nu)
,

(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho^{\prime\prime\prime}_{j},\nu)\in\mathfrak{o}{[{w^{\prime\prime}_{j}}]}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho^{\prime\prime}_{j},\nu)
, and

(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho_{j+1},\nu)\in\mathfrak{o}{[{w^{\prime\prime\prime}_{j}}]}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho^{\prime\prime\prime}_{j},\nu)
, if
j<k
, in particular. Let
\varrho_{i,j}
,
\varrho^{\prime}_{i,j}
,

\varrho^{\prime\prime}_{i,j}
and
\varrho^{\prime\prime\prime}_{i,j}
be the restrictions
of
\varrho_{j}
,
\varrho^{\prime}_{j}
,
\varrho^{\prime\prime}_{j}
and

\varrho^{\prime\prime\prime}_{j}
to
\mathcal{C}_{i}
, for
i=1,2
, respectively. We
prove the following:

1.

\varrho_{2,j}=\varrho^{\prime}_{2,j}
, for all
j\in[{1},{k}]
, and

2.

\varrho^{\prime\prime}_{1,j}=\varrho^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1,j}
, for all
j\in[{1},{k}]
.

We prove the first point, the argument for the second point being
symmetric. It is sufficient to prove that the state of the
components with indices in
\mathcal{C}_{2}
, which are the only ones

\varrho_{2,j}
and
\varrho^{\prime}_{2,j}
account for, is not
changed by
w_{j}
, for all
j\in[{1},{k}]
. Since
w_{j}\in\big({\langle\!\!\langle}{\eta_{1}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{1}})\setminus{\langle\!\!\langle}{\eta_{2}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{2}})\big)^{*}
, the only interactions on

w_{j}
are the ones from
\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{1}=\gamma_{1}\bullet\gamma^{\prime}_{1}
that do not occur in
\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{2}=\gamma_{2}\bullet\gamma^{\prime}_{2}
, where
\gamma^{\prime}_{1}\sqsubseteq\gamma_{2}
and

\gamma^{\prime}_{2}\sqsubseteq\gamma_{1}
. It follows that the
interactions occurring on
w_{j}
are the ones from
\gamma_{1}

that do not occur in
\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{2}
. Since
\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{2}\models\mathop{\scalebox{1.9}{\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$\ast$}}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\alpha\in\mathrm{inter}({\phi_{1}})\setminus(\mathrm{inter}({\overline{\phi}_{1}})\cup\mathrm{inter}({\phi_{2}}))}~\alpha
[
=\mathcal{F}({\phi_{2}},{\phi_{1}})
], the interactions occurring on
w_{j}

must occur in some model
\overline{\gamma}
of a tight
subformula of
\phi_{1}
. Hence, the interactions from

\overline{\gamma}
can only change the state of a component
from
\overline{\gamma}
. Since
\overline{\gamma}\sqsubseteq\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{1}\bullet\gamma_{2}
is defined, there
can be no component indexed by some element of
\mathfrak{C}_{2}
,
whose state is changed by an interaction from

\overline{\gamma}
, thus
\varrho_{2,j}=\varrho^{\prime}_{2,j}

(
1
). Consequently, we obtain two
sequences of words and finite state maps:

–

w_{1},w^{\prime}_{1},w^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1},\ldots,w_{k},w^{\prime}_{k},w^{\prime\prime\prime}_{k}
and

\varrho_{1,1},\varrho^{\prime}_{1,1},\varrho^{\prime\prime}_{1,1},\ldots,\varrho_{1,k},\varrho^{\prime}_{1,k},\varrho^{\prime\prime}_{1,k}
,
where:

*

(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{I}_{1},\varrho^{\prime}_{1,j},\nu)\in\mathfrak{o}{[{w_{1}}]}(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{I}_{1},\varrho_{1,j},\nu)
,

*

(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{I}_{1},\varrho^{\prime\prime}_{1,j},\nu)\in\mathfrak{o}{[{w^{\prime}_{1}}]}(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{I}_{1},\varrho^{\prime}_{1,j},\nu)
and

*

(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{I}_{1},\varrho_{1,j+1},\nu)\in\mathfrak{o}{[{w^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1}}]}(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{I}_{1},\varrho^{\prime\prime}_{1,j},\nu)
,

for all
j\in[{1},{k-1}]
, and

–

w^{\prime}_{1},w^{\prime\prime}_{1},w^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1},\ldots,w^{\prime}_{k},w^{\prime\prime}_{k},w^{\prime\prime\prime}_{k}
and

\varrho^{\prime}_{2,1},\varrho^{\prime\prime}_{2,1},\varrho^{\prime\prime\prime}_{2,1},\ldots,\varrho^{\prime}_{2,k},\varrho^{\prime\prime}_{2,k},\varrho^{\prime\prime\prime}_{2,k}
, where:

*

(\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{I}_{2},\varrho^{\prime\prime}_{2,j},\nu)\in\mathfrak{o}{[{w^{\prime}_{1}}]}(\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{I}_{2},\varrho^{\prime}_{2,j},\nu)
,

*

(\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{I}_{2},\varrho^{\prime\prime\prime}_{2,j},\nu)\in\mathfrak{o}{[{w^{\prime\prime}_{1}}]}(\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{I}_{2},\varrho^{\prime\prime}_{2,j},\nu)

and

*

(\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{I}_{2},\varrho^{\prime}_{2,j+1},\nu)\in\mathfrak{o}{[{w^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1}}]}(\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{I}_{2},\varrho^{\prime\prime\prime}_{2,j},\nu)
,

for all
j\in[{1},{k-1}]
.

Note that
{w}\!\downarrow_{\scriptscriptstyle{{\langle\!\!\langle}{\eta_{1}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{1}})}}=w_{1}w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1}\ldots w_{k}w^{\prime}_{k}w^{\prime\prime\prime}_{k}
and

{w}\!\downarrow_{\scriptscriptstyle{{\langle\!\!\langle}{\eta_{2}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{2}})}}=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime\prime}_{1}w^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1}\ldots w^{\prime}_{k}w^{\prime\prime}_{k}w^{\prime\prime\prime}_{k}
. By the inductive hypothesis, we have

\mathfrak{o}{[{{w}\!\downarrow_{\scriptscriptstyle{{\langle\!\!\langle}{\eta_{i}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{i}})}}}]}(\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{i})\subseteq{{\bf[\![}\psi_{i}*\mathcal{F}({\phi_{i}},{\phi_{3-i}}){\bf]\!]}}
, hence

(\mathcal{C}_{i},\mathcal{I}_{i},\varrho^{\prime\prime\prime}_{i,k},\nu)\in\mathfrak{o}{[{{w}\!\downarrow_{\scriptscriptstyle{{\langle\!\!\langle}{\eta_{i}}{\rangle\!\!\rangle}({\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{i}})}}}]}(\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{i})
,
for
i=1,2
. Moreover,
\mathcal{C}_{1}\uplus\mathcal{C}_{2}=\mathcal{C}
,

\mathcal{I}_{1}\uplus\mathcal{I}_{2}=\mathcal{I}
and the state maps

\varrho^{\prime}
and
\varrho^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1,k}\cup\varrho^{\prime\prime\prime}_{2,k}

agree on all
c\in\mathcal{C}
, hence
\gamma^{\prime}=(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho^{\prime},\nu)\in{{\bf[\![}\psi_{1}*\psi_{2}{\bf]\!]}}
.

Proving (
\star
) for the rest of the rules is a standard check,
left to the reader.
\Box

Appendix C
Proofs from Section
6

See
4

Proof
. Let
\varphi_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}\phi_{i}*\mathop{\scalebox{1.9}{\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$\ast$}}}\hskip 1.0pt_{j=1}^{k_{i}}\mathsf{tree}_{\star}(x_{i,j})*\mathop{\scalebox{1.9}{\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$\ast$}}}\hskip 1.0pt_{j=k_{i}+1}^{\ell_{i}}\mathsf{tseg}(x_{i,j},y_{i,j})
be symbolic configurations,
where
\phi_{i}
is a predicate-free symbolic configuration and

\mathsf{tree}_{\star}(x)
is either
\mathsf{tree}_{\mathit{idle}}(x)
,
\mathsf{tree}_{\neg\mathit{idle}}(x)
or

\mathsf{tree}(x)
, for all
j\in[{1},{\ell_{i}}]
and
i=1,2
. We
prove that
\varphi_{1}
is precise on
{{\bf[\![}\varphi_{2}{\bf]\!]}}
. Let

\gamma=(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{I},\varrho,\nu)\in{{\bf[\![}\varphi_{2}{\bf]\!]}}
be a configuration and
suppose that there exist configurations
\gamma^{\prime}=(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\varrho,\nu),\gamma^{\prime\prime}=(\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{I}^{\prime\prime},\varrho,\nu)
, such that
\gamma^{\prime}\sqsubseteq\gamma
,
\gamma^{\prime\prime}\sqsubseteq\gamma
,
\gamma^{\prime}\models\varphi_{1}
and
\gamma^{\prime\prime}\models\varphi_{1}
. Then there
exist configurations
\gamma^{\prime}_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{0},\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{0},\varrho^{\prime}_{0},\nu),\ldots,\gamma^{\prime}_{\ell_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\ell_{1}},\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{\ell_{1}},\varrho^{\prime}_{\ell_{1}},\nu)

and
\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}_{0},\mathcal{I}^{\prime\prime}_{0},\varrho^{\prime\prime}_{0},\nu),\ldots,\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{\ell_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{def}}}}{{=}}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}_{\ell_{1}},\mathcal{I}^{\prime\prime}_{\ell_{1}},\varrho^{\prime\prime}_{\ell_{1}},\nu)
,
such that:

•

\gamma^{\prime}=\mathop{\scalebox{1.9}{\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$\ast$}}}\hskip 1.0pt_{j=0}^{\ell_{1}}\gamma^{\prime}_{j}
and

\gamma^{\prime\prime}=\mathop{\scalebox{1.9}{\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$\ast$}}}\hskip 1.0pt_{j=0}^{\ell_{1}}\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{j}
,

•

\gamma^{\prime}_{0}\models\phi_{1}
and
\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{0}\models\phi_{1}
,

•

\gamma^{\prime}_{j}\models\mathsf{tree}_{\star}(x_{1,j})
and

\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{j}\models\mathsf{tree}_{\star}(x_{2,j})
, for all
j\in[{1},{k_{1}}]
, and

•

\gamma^{\prime}_{j}\models\mathsf{tseg}(x_{1,j},y_{1,j})
and

\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{j}\models\mathsf{tseg}(x_{2,j},y_{2,j})
, for all
j\in[{k_{1}+1},{\ell_{1}}]
.

Since
\phi_{1}
is a predicate-free symbolic configuration, we have

\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{0}=\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}_{0}
,
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{0}=\mathcal{I}^{\prime\prime}_{0}
and

\varrho^{\prime}_{0}=\varrho^{\prime\prime}_{0}
, thus
\gamma^{\prime}_{0}=\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{0}
. Next, for each
j\in[{1},{k_{1}}]
, we have

\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{j}=\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}_{j}
, because these sets of components
correspond to the vertices of the same tree, whose root is

\nu(x_{1,j})
and whose frontier contains only indices
c\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{j}\cap\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}_{j}
, such that
\varrho(c)\in\{{\mathit{leaf\!\_idle},\mathit{leaf\!\_busy}}\}
. Finally, for each
j\in[{k_{1}+1},{\ell_{1}}]
, we have
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{j}=\mathcal{I}^{\prime\prime}_{j}
,
because these sets of interactions correspond to the edges of the
same tree, whose root is
\nu(x_{1,j})
and whose frontier
contains
\nu(y_{1,j})
together with indices
c\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{j}\cap\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}_{j}
, such that
\varrho(c)\in\{{\mathit{leaf\!\_idle},\mathit{leaf\!\_busy}}\}
. We obtain, consequently, that

\gamma^{\prime}_{j}=\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{j}
, for all
j\in[{1},{\ell_{1}}]
,
leading to
\gamma^{\prime}=\gamma^{\prime\prime}
.
\Box

Appendix D
Havoc Invariance Proofs from Section
6

In order to shorten the following proofs, we introduce the rule
(
\mathsf{I}
\dagger
) that allows us to remove a disabled interaction atom

\alpha
from the pre- and postcondition, the environment and the
language if certain conditions hold.

Lemma 4

Using the notation in §
5
, the following rule is sound:

{\eta\setminus\{{\Sigma[{\alpha}]}\}}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi}\}\!\!\}

(
\mathsf{I}
\dagger
)

\begin{array}[]{l}\scriptstyle{\alpha=\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle}\\[-1.42262pt]
\scriptstyle{\Sigma[{\alpha}]\in\eta\setminus\mathrm{supp}({\mathsf{L}})}\\[-1.42262pt]
\scriptstyle{{\phi}\dagger{\alpha}.}\end{array}

{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi*\alpha}\}\!\!\}~{\Sigma[{\alpha}]\cup\mathsf{L}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi*\alpha}\}\!\!\}

Proof
. We assume that
\phi
and
\psi
are two symbolic
configurations,
\eta
is an environment and
\alpha=\langle{{x_{1}}.{\mathit{p_{1}}},{x_{2}}.{\mathit{p_{2}}}}\rangle
an interaction
atom. Furthermore,
\Sigma[{\alpha}]\in\eta\setminus\mathrm{supp}({\mathsf{L}})
and
{\phi}\dagger{\alpha}
. Then we can apply
the rule (
\cup
) first and the rules (
\mathsf{C}
), (
\Sigma
) and
(
\mathsf{I}-
) on the subtrees and obtain:

(
\dagger
)

{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi*\alpha}\}\!\!\}~{\Sigma[{\alpha}]}~\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{false}}\}\!\!\}

(
\mathsf{C}
)

{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi*\alpha}\}\!\!\}~{\Sigma[{\alpha}]}~\{\!\!\{{\psi*\alpha}\}\!\!\}

{\eta\setminus\{{\Sigma[{\alpha}]}\}}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi}\}\!\!\}

(
\mathsf{I}-
)

{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi*\alpha}\}\!\!\}~{\mathsf{L}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi*\alpha}\}\!\!\}

(
\cup
)

{\eta}\triangleright\{\!\!\{{\phi*\alpha}\}\!\!\}~{\Sigma[{\alpha}]\cup\mathsf{L}}~\{\!\!\{{\psi*\alpha}\}\!\!\}
.

Hence the rule can by derived from the rules in Fig.
5
.

D.2
Havoc Invariance of the Predicate Atom
\mathsf{tree}_{\mathit{idle}}(x)

The invariance of the predicate
\mathsf{tree}_{\mathit{idle}}(x)
is proven via the rules in
Fig.
5
and the proof is structured similar to the previous
invariance proof.

(
\epsilon
)

{\emptyset}\triangleright~\{\!\!\{{{x}@{\mathit{leaf\!\_idle}}}\}\!\!\}

{\epsilon}~\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{tree}_{\mathit{idle}}(x)}\}\!\!\}
